## Page 1


Quotients of real algebraic sets and AS-sets equipped with a
linearizable compact group action
Fabien Priziac
Abstract
We present the full-detailed construction of new geometric quotient structures for affine
real algebraic varieties equipped with a linearizable action of a compact Lie group G. These
quotients are functors from the category of arc-symmetric/AS-sets equipped with a lineariz-
able action of G and equivariant continuous maps with AS-graph to the category of AS-sets.
We furthermore provide a complete review of the results employed in the constructions, in-
cluding properties of equivariant real algebraic geometry with respect to polynomial group
actions, as well as an introduction to semialgebraic arc-symmetric sets and AS-sets of
compact affine Nash manifolds.

Introduction
In this text, we deal with real algebraic sets equipped with some action of a compact Lie
group G: a real algebraic set of a real affine space Rn is the zero set of a polynomial with real
coefficients. We consider more generally the semialgebraic arc-symmetric sets of Rn or of a
compact Nash submanifold of Rn, as well as their finite Boolean combinations called AS-sets.
A semialgebraic subset of Rn is a finite union of sets of Rn defined by polynomial equalities and
inequalities, a Nash submanifold of Rn is a semialgebraic analytic submanifold of Rn having
analytic charts with semialgebraic data, while an arc-symmetric set of Rn (this notion has been
defined by K. Kurdyka in [15]) is a subset S of Rn such that for any analytic arc with values
in Rn, either the arc meets S at isolated points, either the arc is entirely contained in S.
Our goal is to construct and study some functorial quotients, with respect to the considered
actions of G, with a semialgebraic arc-symmetric structure, generalizing one part of our work
in [20]. In order to give the quotient by G this geometric structure, our main tool will be
the real algebro-geometric quotient by G. That is why we consider compact real algebraic
groups with polynomial group structure (have in mind that a compact Lie group has, up to an
isomorphism of Lie groups, such a representative: see remark 6.3.10 of the present document
and section 2.5 of Chapter 5 of [19]) and their polynomial actions on real algebraic sets (these
actions are all linearizable up to equivariant polynomial isomorphism, hence the title of this
document). Indeed, if G is such a compact real algebraic group, Hilbert’s finiteness theorem
is valid and allows, together with Real Nullstellensatz, to associate to any G-real algebraic set
a G-invariant polynomial map which restricts into the topological quotient map (contrary to
the complex case, this real algebro-geometric quotient map is not surjective in general).
We chose to explain the construction of our AS-quotients in full details, as a self-contained
pedagogical introduction to the subject for any reader being a little familiar with real algebraic
sets and semialgebraic sets. In particular, we will show most part of the employed results.
A key new result is theorem 7.3.2 which asserts notably that, if G is a compact real algebraic
group, the semialgebraic quotient set of a G-real algebraic set on which G acts freely is an
arc-symmetric set of a real affine space. From theorem 7.3.2 and its generalization to AS-
sets of Rn (theorem 7.3.5), all statements of section 8 are original, while the first part of
this document is dedicated to the proofs of preliminaries that lead to theorem 7.3.2 as well as
surrounding properties, that are interesting on their own. This includes for instance a complete
demonstration of Hilbert’s finiteness theorem in the real case and a review of specific features
of compact real algebraic groups. Even if statements of sections 2 to 7 may reveal classical,
well-known or else true in the complex case, some of them were hard or impossible to find in
the literature: this was another motivation to write down those arguments, for future reference.
In order to preserve the flow of the arguments, some statements and proofs have been gath-
ered in an appendix. In the last part of this appendix, we give in particular an introduction to
arc-symmetric sets and AS-sets. These classes of objects generalize in some sense real algebraic
sets and closed analytic sets, keeping enough rigidity while allowing more flexibility. Following
3


## Page 4


the seminal papers [15] and [16], we first state definitions and properties about arc-symmetric
sets of Rn together with full-detailed proofs. As for arc-symmetric sets in more general spaces,
we decided to place ourselves in the framework of Nash manifolds. In a second time, we re-
strict to compact affine Nash manifolds and justify properties and characterizations of section
3 of [16] about arc-symmetric sets and AS-sets of Pn(R). Again, our intention is to provide a
self-contained exhaustive review of the results that we need in sections 7 and 8, as well as to
emphasize the importance of arc-symmetric sets and AS-sets in real algebraic geometry.
Let us now describe, in a more developed way, the different parts of this document, with
this aim of constructing functorial AS-quotients for any real algebraic set equipped with a
polynomial action of a compact group.
As we said above, the fundamental tool that we use is the real geometric quotient which
results from a combination of Hilbert’s finiteness theorem and Real Nullstellensatz.
These
theorems impose to consider groups that are real algebraic sets and have polynomial group
structure (we call such a group a real algebraic group: definition 2.1.1) and, if G is such a real
algebraic group, real algebraic sets X equipped with a polynomial action α : G × X →X of
G (we call such a set a G-real algebraic set: definition 3.0.1). Section 2 is dedicated to the
former, while section 3 is dedicated to the latter. Notice that the polynomial action of G on a
G-real algebraic set X can always be linearized (proposition 3.0.8): there exists an equivariant
polynomial isomomorphism from X to G-real algebraic subset of a polynomial representation
RN of G (definition 3.0.4: the linear action of G on RN is given by a matrix with polynomial
coordinates in g ∈G).
Let X be a G-real algebraic set. The R-algebra P(X) of polynomial functions on X can be
equipped with an induced linear action of G, by right composition with the action, and we can
consider the sub-algebra P(X)G of G-invariant polynomial functions on X. If we assume that
the real algebraic group G is linearly reductive (definition 4.1.1, see also the characterizations
of proposition 4.1.3), according to Hilbert’s finiteness theorem (theorem 4.2.1), the R-algebra
P(X)G is finitely generated: we dedicate section 4 to these notion and result. The equivalence
of categories provided by Real Nullstellensatz (theorem 5.1.1) then asserts that the inclusion
morphism P(X)G ,→P(X) corresponds to a polynomial map πX : X →X//G that we call
the geometric quotient map of X (definition 5.1.3). This geometric quotient is functorial with
respect to equivariant polynomial maps between G-real algebraic sets and is compatible with
equivariant inclusion: see lemmas 5.2.1 and 5.2.3 of section 5.
The above construction is analog to the construction of the geometric quotient map in the
complex case and, due to the polynomial hypothesis, the real geometric quotient map πX of X
can be complexified into the complex geometric quotient πXC of its complexification XC by the
complexified action of GC (see lemma 3.0.13 and subsection 5.4). From another point of view,
πX is induced by πXC: we will also take advantage of this facts.
On the other hand, differences have to be noticed between the real and complex versions of
the above notions and constructions. For instance, contrary to the complex case, the image of a
morphism of real algebraic groups is not necessarily a real algebraic group (remark 2.1.5 1) and
the image of the real geometric quotient map is not necessarily surjective (see examples 5.1.5).
Nevertheless, real algebraic geometry comes with an extra feature, namely semialgebraic sets.
4


## Page 5


This allows to consider the image of a morphism of real algebraic groups as a semialgebraic
group (definition 2.2.1), while the image of a real geometric quotient map is always a semial-
gebraic set.
From section 6, we focus on polynomial (or, equivariantly, linearizable) actions of compact
real algebraic groups. If G is a compact real algebraic group, the associated Haar measure
allows to define a Reynolds operator for G (definition 4.1.2 and example 4.1.7 2) and G is
therefore a linearly reductive real algebraic group (corollary 4.1.5). As a consequence, we can
consider the functorial geometric quotient of G-real algebraic sets.
A fundamental property of separation of orbits (lemma 6.1.1) also allows to show that the
image of a compact real algebraic group by a morphism of real algebraic groups is a (compact)
real algebraic group.
Moreover, polar decomposition can be used to prove that a bijective
morphism of real algebraic groups between compact real algebraic groups (in fact any bijective
continuous group homomorphism between compact real algebraic groups) is an isomorphism
(corollary 6.3.6 and example 6.3.9 3, see also theorem 6.3.3 as well as proposition 6.2.2 that
asserts that any compact real algebraic group is isomorphic to a real algebraic subgroup of an
orthogonal group ON(R)).
This draws a parallel between the behaviors of compact real algebraic groups and of com-
plex algebraic groups. But we have actually more: if X is a G-real algebraic set, the orbit of
any point of X by the action of G is a real algebraic set (proposition 6.1.2) and the restric-
tion ϖX : X →πX(X) of the geometric quotient of X onto its semialgebraic image (called the
semialgebraic quotient of X: definition 5.1.8) is the topological quotient of X by G (propo-
sition 6.4.4). We thereafter show the functoriality of semialgebraic quotient with respect to
semialgebraic (resp. continuous, resp. proper) maps (proposition 6.4.9).
As we can see through examples 5.1.5, the semialgebraic quotient set X/G := πX(X) ⊂Rr
of a G-real algebraic set X is not even an AS-set of Rr in general. Nevertheless, if the action of G
on X is free, then the set X/G is arc-symmetric in Rr (theorem 7.3.2). This is a consequence
of theorem 7.2.2 which asserts that a nonsingular point x of X with trivial stabilizer is sent
onto a nonsingular point in dimension dim X −dim G of X//G. This fact implies the existence
of a local Nash section for the geometric quotient map πX (theorem 7.2.4) which allows to
lift some analytic arcs on X//G passing through πX(x). The same technique allows to show
further functorialities of the semialgebraic quotient, for instance its functoriality with respect
to equivariant arc-analytic maps defined on G-real algebraic sets on which G acts freely: see
proposition 7.2.8.
Let us also highlight a key property that is used to prove theorem 7.2.2. If x is a point
of X such that Gx = {e}, then the bijective polynomial map which associates to any g ∈G the
element g · x of the real algebraic orbit of x is actually a polynomial isomorphism. The latter
then induces an isomorphism of R-algebras P(G · x) →P(G) which reveals crucial in the proof
of theorem 7.2.2.
In section 8, we consider more generally AS-sets of compact affine Nash manifolds. In view
of section 7, we consider, in a first time (subsections 8.1 and 8.2), AS-sets that can be seen as
a G-stable AS-set of a compact Nash submanifold N of a polynomial representation Rd of G
5


## Page 6


with the extra hypothesis that the action of G on the AS-closure in N is free: see definition
8.2.1 for the precise definition of a free G-AS-set.
Any such free G-AS-set gives rise to a well-defined AS-quotient: with the above notations, if
S is a G-stable AS-set of N such that the action of G on S
AS is free, the set S/G := πRd(S) ⊂Rr
is an AS-set of Pr(R) and the restriction ϱS : S →S/G of πRd is a (continuous) AS-map,
i.e. a map with AS-graph. This AS-quotient (definition 8.3.2) is functorial with respect to
equivariant continuous maps with AS-graph (theorem 8.3.3) and compatible with equivariant
closed inclusions (proposition 8.3.5).
Let X be a G-real algebraic set of a real affine space Rn. Though the action of G on X
is linearizable by proposition 3.0.8 (and even orthogonalizable since G is compact: see propo-
sition 6.2.1), X is not a free G-AS-set of Pn(R) in general. However, if S is a free G-AS-set,
the Cartesian product X × S (equipped with the diagonal action of G) is a free G-AS-set
as well (proposition 8.2.6, see also proposition 8.2.2) and we can consider the AS-quotient
ϱX×S : X × S →(X × S)/G, that we call the relative AS-quotient of X with respect to S
(definition 8.4.1). This relative quotient by G is a bifunctor with respect, in both entries, to
equivariant continuous AS-maps.
The latter construction will be the starting point of a subsequent paper where we will gen-
eralize the second part of [20]. More precisely, if G is a compact real algebraic group, we will
apply an, in some sense, additive homological functor (namely the Nash-constructible filtered
chain complex defined and studied by C. McCrory and A. Parusi´nski in section 3 of [17]) to a
sequence of relative AS-quotients by G associated to any G-AS-set (definition 8.2.1). We will
then deduce additive numerical invariants of G-AS-sets, in particular of G-real algebraic sets
and G-stable Zariski-open subsets of G-real algebraic sets. Because the relative AS-quotient
of G is (bi)functorial with respect to equivariant continuous AS-maps, the invariants that we
will define will be invariant with respect to equivariant homeomorphisms with AS-graph.
Let us finally introduce notations that we will use all along this text.
We will first denote by R[x1, . . . , xn], resp. C[x1, . . . , xn], the R-algebra, resp. C-algebra, of
polynomials in n indeterminates x1, . . . , xn with real, resp. complex, coefficients. If E is any
subset of the ring of functions from Rn to R, resp. Cn to C, we denote by V (E), resp. V(E), the
subset of points x of Rn, resp. Cn, satisfying f(x) = 0 for all f ∈E. If S is any subset of Rn,
resp. Cn, we denote by I(S), resp. I(S), the ideal of polynomials P of the ring R[x1, . . . , xn],
resp. C[x1, . . . , xn], such that P(x) = 0 for any x ∈S. The Zariski closure of S, i.e. the
smallest real, resp.
complex, algebraic set of Rn, resp.
Cn, containing S, will be denoted
by S

In addition, if K is a commutative field, the zero vector of a K-vector space V will be
in general denoted by 0, while L(V ), resp. GL(V ), will denote the K-vector space of linear
endomorphisms, resp. automorphisms, of V . The zero, resp. identity, matrix of Mn(K) will
be denoted by 0n, resp. In, and, when this is relevant, we will identify any square matrix
(aij)1≤i,j≤n of Mn(K) as a vector of Kn2.
Moreover, if i, j ∈{1, . . . , n}, we will use the
Kronecker delta symbol δij which is equal to 1 if i = j and 0 otherwise, and if λ1, . . . , λn are
scalars, we will write Diag(λ1, . . . , λn) to denote the diagonal matrix of Mn(K) with diagonal
coefficients λ1, . . . , λn in this order.
6


## Page 7


To conclude, let us also mention that the C∞, analytic or Nash manifolds we consider
are all Hausdorff, that a smooth map means a C∞map and that a (sub)manifold means
a C∞(sub)manifold. In addition, a regular map between Zariski-open subsets of algebraic sets
(i.e. set-theoritic differences of algebraic sets of affine spaces) means a map whose coordinate
functions are well-defined quotients of polynomial functions, and a biregular map is a bijective
regular map with regular inverse (contrary to the real case, a regular map defined on a complex
algebraic set is always the restriction of a polynomial map: see theorem 9.1.2 of the appendix).

2
Real algebraic groups with polynomial structure maps
2.1
First definitions and properties of real algebraic groups
In the first part of this text, we will be considering real algebraic groups with polynomial group
structure i.e.
whose structure maps are given by restriction of polynomial maps (and not
maps that are just regular). Our motivation to make this hypothesis is our subsequent use
of Hilbert’s finiteness theorem (theorem 4.2.1 below) in order to consider algebro-geometric
quotient of real algebraic sets acted by compact real algebraic group action. Indeed, Hilbert’s
finiteness theorem requires that both the involved group and its action are given by polynomial
maps.
Let m be a positive integer.
Definition 2.1.1. Let G be a real algebraic subset of Rm and let µ : G × G →G be a binary
operation such that the pair (G, µ) is a group. Let us furthermore denote by ω the inverse map
G →G ; g 7→g−1.
We say that G is a real algebraic group if both maps µ and ω are (restrictions of) polynomial
maps.
If G is a real algebraic group and H is a real algebraic subset of Rm as well as a subgroup
of G, then we say that H is a real algebraic subgroup of G (in this case, H is a real algebraic
group too). As first examples, let us mention the groups that will follow us along this paper:
let n be a positive integer.
Examples 2.1.2.
1. Consider the real orthogonal group On(R) of orthogonal matrices of Mn(R) as being the
real algebraic subset of points (aij)1≤i,j≤n of Rn2 such that

(the inverse map of the group is (x, y) ∈K 7→(y, x) ∈K).
4. If G ⊂Rm and H ⊂RM are real algebraic groups, the direct product group G × H ⊂
Rm+M is a real algebraic group.
Before stating some basic geometrical results on real algebraic groups, let us make precise
the notions of morphism and isomorphism that we will be considering on these objects:
Definition 2.1.3. Let G ⊂Rm and H ⊂RM be real algebraic groups and let φ : G →H
be a map. We say that φ is a morphism of real algebraic groups if φ is the restriction of a
polynomial map Rm →RM as well as a group homomorphism.
If φ is a bijective morphism of real algebraic groups and its inverse φ−1 is also a morphism
of real algebraic groups (i.e. the restriction of a polynomial map RM →Rm, since the inverse
map of a bijective group homomorphism is a group homomorphism as well), we will say that φ
is an isomorphism of real algebraic groups and that G and H are isomorphic as real algebraic
groups.
In the following, a bijective restriction of a polynomial map between real algebraic sets
whose inverse is also the restriction of a polynomial map will also be called a polynomial
isomorphism (and we will say that the source and target of a polynomial isomorphism are
polynomially isomorphic real algebraic sets): an isomorphism of real algebraic groups is a
group homomorphism between real algebraic groups which is a polynomial isomorphism.

tion which associates to any points (x, y) and (x′, y′) of S1 the couple (xx′−yy′, xy′+x′y),

and is therefore an isomorphism of real algebraic groups (the inverse map φ−1 is the
restriction of an analogous polynomial map).
Remarks 2.1.5.
1. If φ : G →H is a morphism of real algebraic groups, then the kernel of φ, or more
generally the inverse image by φ of any real algebraic subgroup of H, is always a real
algebraic subgroup of G, since the inverse image of a real algebraic set by a polynomial
map is a real algebraic set. However, the image of a morphism of real algebraic groups
is not a real algebraic group in general, contrary to the complex case (see for instance
Proposition 21.2.4 of [26]). Indeed, consider for example the real algebraic group K :=

(x, y) ∈R2 | xy = 1
	
(see example 2.1.2 3) and the endomorphism ψ : (x, y) ∈K 7→
(x2, y2) ∈K of K: the image of ψ is the subgroup

(x, y) ∈R2 | xy = 1, x > 0, y > 0
	
of K, which is not a real algebraic set.
2. Contrary to the complex case (see Proposition 21.2.6 of [26]), a bijective morphism of real
algebraic groups is not an isomorphism of real algebraic groups in general: if we consider
again the above real algebraic group K, the inverse map of the bijective endomorphism
of real algebraic group (x, y) ∈K 7→(x3, y3) ∈K is not the restriction of a polynomial
map.
In the following, let G ⊂Rm be a real algebraic group and denote by µ its polynomial
structure map, by ω its associated polynomial inverse map and by e its identity element.
We are going to state two general geometrical properties of real algebraic groups, which are
analogous to the complex case. We will give their proofs, for the sake of self-containedness.
Proposition 2.1.6. There is a unique irreducible component G0 of the real algebraic set G
containing e. Furthermore, G0 is a real algebraic subgroup of G, which is normal and of finite
index as a subgroup of G, and the cosets of G modulo G0 are the irreducible components of the
real algebraic set G.
9


## Page 10


Proof. We follow the proofs of Lemma 21.1.4 and Theorem 21.1.6 of [26]. Let first G1 and G2
be two irreducible components of the real algebraic set G containing e and let us show that
G1 = G2, following the proof of Lemma 21.1.4 of [26]. The product G1 × G2 is an irreducible
real algebraic subset of Rm × Rm, so that µ(G1 × G2) is an irreducible subset of G equipped
with its Zariski topology since the polynomial map µ is in particular continuous with respect
to Zariski topology (see Proposition 1.1.7 (i) of [26]). But µ(G1 × G2) contains the irreducible
components G1 = µ(G1 × {e}) and G2 = µ({e} × G2) of G, and therefore, by maximality,
G1 = µ(G1 × G2) = G2.
So now denote by G0 the unique irreducible component of G containing e. By the above
arguments, µ(G0 × G0) = G0 and, furthermore, ω(G0) = G0 because, since ω : G →G is a
polynomial isomorphism and ω(e) = e, ω(G0) is an irreducible component of G containing e.
As a consequence, G0 is a real algebraic subgroup of G.
In order to show that G0 is a normal subgroup of G, if g ∈G, denote by lg the left translation
map h ∈G 7→µ(g, h) ∈G and by rg the right translation map h ∈G 7→µ(h, g) ∈G: lg
and rg are both polynomial isomorphisms (of respective inverse maps lω(g) and rω(g)). For
all g ∈G, the image lg ◦rω(g)(G0) is therefore an irreducible component of G containing e so
that lg ◦rω(g)(G0) = G0, and G0 is then a normal subgroup of G.
Finally, the group G is the disjoint union of its cosets lg(G0), g ∈G, but, for all g ∈G, lg(G0)
is an irreducible component of the real algebraic set G containing g and any real algebraic set is
the finite union of its irreducible components, hence the last statements of the proposition.
Remarks 2.1.7.
1. Any irreducible real algebraic set is Zariski-connected but the converse is not true. How-
ever, by previous proposition 2.1.6, any real algebraic group is irreducible if and only if
it is Zariski-connected (because the irreducible components of a real algebraic group do
not intersect).
2. A real algebraic group which is connected with respect to Euclidean topology is nec-
essarily irreducible as a real algebraic set (since the irreducible components of a real
algebraic group do not intersect and any real algebraic set is a closed set with respect to
Euclidean topology), while the converse is not true, as illustrated by the real algebraic
group

(x, y) ∈R2 | xy = 1
	
(example 2.1.2 3).
We remarked that the irreducible components of the real algebraic group G do not intersect
(notice also that they are all polynomially isomorphic to one another via the translation maps
of G). Actually, G does not have any singular point :
Lemma 2.1.8. The real algebraic group G is a nonsingular real algebraic set.
Proof. Let g be a nonsingular point of G (such a point always exists by, for instance, Proposition
3.3.14 of [3]) and let h ∈G. We have h = lµ(h,ω(g))(g) and lµ(h,ω(g)) is a polynomial isomorphism
(in particular, a biregular isomorphism), so that h is a nonsingular point of G as well.
In particular, G is a Nash submanifold of Rm (see Proposition 3.3.11 and Definition 2.9.9 of
[3]) and then, since the polynomial structure maps µ and ω of the group G are in particular Nash
10


## Page 11


maps (i.e. C∞maps with semialgebraic graphs), G is an affine Nash group (see for instance [12]).
This implies that G is a Lie group whose dimension (the dimension of G as a C∞submanifold
of Rm coincides with its dimension as a real algebraic variety: see Proposition 2.8.14 of [3]) can
be computed as the dimension, as an R-vector space, of the associated Lie algebra. For instance,
the Lie groups On(R) and SOn(R) have the same Lie algebra {M ∈Mn(R) | tM = −M}, so
that the real algebraic groups On(R) and SOn(R) have dimension n(n−1)
2
.
2.2
Semialgebraic groups with polynomial structure maps
In this part, motivated by the example of the images of morphisms of real algebraic groups
(cf. remark 2.1.5 1), we want to extend to the larger context of semialgebraic subgroups of real
algebraic groups:
Definition 2.2.1. Let G be a semialgebraic subset of Rm and let µ : G × G →G be a binary
operation such that the pair (G, µ) is a group.
If ω denotes furthermore the inverse map
G →G ; g 7→g−1, we say that G is a semialgebraic group if both maps µ and ω are restrictions
of polynomial maps.
Examples 2.2.2.
1. As first examples, real algebraic groups are of course semialgebraic groups, as well as the
image of any morphism of real algebraic groups (since the image of a semialgebraic set by
a polynomial map is a semialgebraic set: see Proposition 2.2.7 of [3]): for instance, the
image

(x, y) ∈R2 | xy = 1, x > 0, y > 0
	
of the endomorphism of real algebraic group
(x, y) ∈K 7→(x2, y2) ∈K, where K =

(x, y) ∈R2 | xy = 1
	
, is a semialgebraic group.
2. Remark that, more generally, the kernel and the image of a map between semialgebraic
groups which is both a group homomorphism and a semialgebraic map (i.e. which has
a semialgebraic graph) are semialgebraic groups, since inverse image or image of any
semialgebraic subset by a semialgebraic map is always a semialgebraic set (see again
Proposition 2.2.7 of [3]).
Let G ⊂Rm be a semialgebraic group. As we suggested in the introduction of this sub-

induced from a real algebraic group structure on the Zariski closure G

Let us carry on this discussion. Actually, the semialgebraic group G is not any semialgebraic
subset of its Zariski closure G

connected components, which are all semialgebraic (cf. Theorem 2.4.5 of [3]).
Lemma 2.2.5. There is a unique connected component Gc of the semialgebraic set G contain-
ing e. Furthermore, Gc is a semialgebraic subgroup of G, which is normal and of finite index as
a subgroup of G, and the cosets of G modulo Gc are the (semialgebraic) connected components
of the semialgebraic set G.
Proof. Just adapt the proof of proposition 2.1.6 by replacing the word “irreducible” with the
word “connected” (with respect to the Euclidean topology of Rm): a polynomial map, being
continuous, carries connected sets to connected sets and a polynomial isomorphism, being a
homeomorphism, carries connected components to connected components.
Remark 2.2.6. Apart from the semialgebraicity and the finiteness of the index of Gc as a
subgroup of G, the other properties of lemma 2.2.5 just come from the fact that a semialgebraic
group is a topological group.

2.3
Complexification of real algebraic groups
Considering real algebraic groups with polynomial group structure allows to consider the com-
plexification of the latter, as we are going to explain below. A real algebraic group structure
can then also be seen as being induced from a complex algebraic group structure given by
polynomial maps with real coefficients.
13


## Page 14


Recall that, if X is a real algebraic subset of Rm ⊂Cm, the complexification of X, denoted
by XC, is the complex Zariski closure X
Z of X in Cm i.e. the set V(I(X)) of points of Cm
at which vanishes any polynomial of C[x1, . . . , xm] that vanishes at all points of X. We have
X = XC ∩Rd (since X = V (I(X)) and, if x is any point of XC with real coordinates, any
polynomial of R[x1, . . . , xm] vanishing at all points of X vanishes at x) and, if f : X →Y is
the restriction of a polynomial map ef : Rm →RM and if efC : Cm →CM denotes map whose
coordinate functions are given by the same polynomial expressions as the coordinate functions
of ef, then efC (XC) ⊂YC, so that we can extend f into the restriction fC : XC →YC of efC
(such an extension is unique: two polynomial maps coinciding on X coincide on XC). Let us
also recall that the complexification of real algebraic sets and (restrictions of) polynomial maps
between real algebraic sets is functorial.
Now, let G ⊂Rm be a real algebraic group with structure map µ : G×G →G, inverse map
ω : G ⊂Rm →G ⊂Rm and neutral element e, and consider the respective complexifications
GC ⊂Cm and µC : GC × GC →GC of G and µ.
Lemma 2.3.1. The couple (GC, µC) is a complex algebraic group, i.e. a complex algebraic set
equipped with a polynomial (or, equivalently, regular) group structure, with inverse map ωC and
neutral element e.
Proof. The proof is analogous to the proof of lemma 2.2.3 (see also remark 2.2.4).
Remarks 2.3.2.
1. By the Hilbert’s Nullstellensatz, a regular function (i.e. well-defined quotient of poly-
nomial maps) on a complex algebraic set is necessarily the restriction of a polynomial
function (see for instance theorem 9.1.2 in the appendix). This is not the case for regular
functions on real algebraic sets.
2. With the above notations, if the ideal I(X) of R[x1, . . . , xm] is generated by polynomials
P1, . . . , Pk (and X is then the set V (P1, . . . , Pk) of points of Rm at which vanish the poly-
nomials P1, . . . , Pk), then the ideal I(X) of C[x1, . . . , xm] is also generated by the polyno-
mials P1, . . . , Pk (because a polynomial P +iQ ∈C[x1, . . . , xm], with P, Q ∈R[x1, . . . , xm],
vanishes on X if and only if P and Q vanish on X) and then the complexification XC
is the set V(P1, . . . , Pk) of points of Cm at which vanish the polynomials P1, . . . , Pk. In
particular, XC is defined by polynomial equations with real coefficients and can then be
considered as an affine algebraic variety over R. In our context, GC can be considered as
an affine algebraic group over R. Notice also that if x is a point of XC, then the point x
of Cm whose coordinates are the respective conjugates of the coordinates of x belongs
to XC as well.
3. For general properties on complex algebraic sets and complex algebraic groups, we refer
for instance to [26].
Before giving some examples of complexifications of real algebraic groups, let us state a
property which is an application of the following fact: if X a real algebraic subset of Rm and X =
X1 ∪· · ·∪XN is its decomposition into irreducible components, then XC = (X1)C ∪· · ·∪(XN)C
14

irreducible real algebraic set and if XC is a union of complex algebraic sets Z1, Z2 of Cm, then
X = XC ∩Rm = (Z1 ∩Rm) ∪(Z2 ∩Rm) so that, without loss of generality, X = Z1 ∩Rm and
then XC ⊂Z1).
Lemma 2.3.3. Let (GC)0 denote the unique irreducible component of the complex algebraic
group GC containing e (cf. Lemma 21.1.4 of [26]). Then (G0)C = (GC)0.
Proof. Since G0 is an irreducible component of G, (G0)C is an irreducible component of GC:
since (G0)C contains e, by uniqueness, we have (G0)C = (GC)0:

Lemma 2.3.5. Let φ : G ⊂Rm →H ⊂RM be a morphism of real algebraic groups. Then the

νC (φC(·), φC(·)) on GC × GC.

As we already mentioned in remark 2.1.5, a bijective morphism of complex algebraic groups
is an isomorphism of complex algebraic groups (see Proposition 21.2.6 of [26]), while this is
not the case for morphisms of real algebraic groups. On the other hand, a morphism of real
algebraic groups whose complexification is bijective is an isomorphism of real algebraic groups,
thanks to the following fact:
Lemma 2.3.7. Let f : X ⊂Rm →Y ⊂RM be a (restriction of a) polynomial map between real
algebraic sets. Then f is a polynomial isomorphism if and only if fC : XC ⊂Cm →YC ⊂CM
is a polynomial isomorphism.
Proof. We give the proof of this equivalence for the sake of completeness. The direct implication
is given by the functoriality of complexification.
As for the converse implication, suppose
that fC has a polynomial inverse map g : YC ⊂CM →XC ⊂Cm. Take a point y ∈Y ⊂YC
and consider its image x := g(y) ∈XC by g, as well as the conjugated point x ∈XC of x.
Since fC(x) = y, because the coordinate functions of fC are given by polynomials with real
coefficients and y has real coordinates, we have fC(x) = fC(x) and then x = x since the map fC
is injective. As a consequence, x = g(y) ∈Rm ∩XC = X. In particular, the imaginary parts of
the polynomial coordinate functions of g vanish on Y and then on YC: the map g is therefore
the restriction of a polynomial map CM →Cm given by polynomials with real coefficients. The
restriction Y ⊂RM →X ⊂Rm of g is consequently a real polynomial inverse map for f.
16


## Page 17


Corollary 2.3.8. Let φ : G →H be a morphism of real algebraic groups.
Then φ is an
isomorphism of real algebraic groups if and only if φC : GC →HC is bijective.
Proof. By lemma 2.3.5, φC is a morphism of complex algebraic groups. Therefore, by Propo-
sition 21.2.6 of [26], φC is a polynomial isomorphism if and only if φC is bijective, hence the
result by previous lemma 2.3.7.
3
Polynomial actions of real algebraic groups on real algebraic
sets
We are now going to introduce the frame we intend to work with, namely the real algebraic sets
on which a real algebraic group (in the sense of definition 2.1.1) acts polynomially. Again, our
motivation to restrict ourselves, at first, to polynomial actions is to be able to apply Hilbert’s
finiteness theorem (theorem 4.2.1).
Let m and n denote positive integers and let G ⊂Rm be a real algebraic group with identity
element e.
Definition 3.0.1. Let X be a real algebraic subset of Rn. We say that X is a G-real algebraic
set if X is equipped with a group action α : G × X →X ; (g, x) 7→g · x of G such that the
map α is (the restriction of) a polynomial map.
Remark that if X ⊂Rn is a G-real algebraic set and if Y is a real algebraic subset of Rn
included in X and globally preserved by the considered action α of G on X (i.e. for all g ∈G,
for all y ∈Y , g · y = α(g, y) ∈Y ), then Y is a G-real algebraic set as well: in this case, we will
say that Y is a G-real algebraic subset of X.
Notice also that if φ : H →G is morphism of real algebraic groups, then we can consider
the polynomial action H × X →X ; (h, x) 7→α(φ(h), x) of H on X, making X into a H-real
algebraic set as well.
Let us give a first list of examples of G-real algebraic sets:
Examples 3.0.2.
1. Matrix multiplication makes the entire affine space Rn into a Ωn(R)-real algebraic set, as
well as a On(R)-real algebraic set and a SOn(R)-real algebraic set.
2. The cone of equation x2 + y2 = z2 in R3 as well as the set of equation x2 + y2 + z4 = z2
are real algebraic subsets of R3 globally preserved by the polynomial action
S1 × R3
→
R3
 (a, b), (x, y, z)

7→
(ax −by, bx + ay, z)
of S1 on R3.

vectors of Rn (with respect to the canonical scalar product) i.e. the real algebraic subset

Let us then emphasize an important class of G-real algebraic sets:
Definition 3.0.4. Let V be an n-dimensional real representation of G i.e. an n-dimensional
real vector space with a group homomorphism ρ : G →GL(V ). Consider a basis of V and
denote by (v1, . . . , vn) the corresponding coordinate system. If the action G×V →V ; (g, v) 7→
ρ(g)(v) is polynomial with respect to the coordinates (v1, . . . , vn) and the coordinates of the
affine space Rm (i.e. if, for all g ∈G, the entries of the matrix of ρ(g) in the considered basis
of V are polynomial in the coordinates of g; notice that this property is independent of the
chosen basis of V ), we say that V is a polynomial representation of G.
Examples 3.0.5.
1. Considering the inclusion ρ : Ωn(R) ,→GL(Rn), the affine space Rn is a polynomial
representation of Ωn(R), as well as of On(R) and SOn(R).

G →GL(V ).

Below, we will state that, up to isomorphism, any G-real algebraic set can actually be
considered as a G-real algebraic subset of a polynomial representation of G. But we first have
to make precise what notion of isomorphism of G-real algebraic sets we will be considering:
Definition 3.0.6. Let X ⊂Rn and Y ⊂RN be G-real algebraic sets and let f : X →Y be a
map. We say that f is a morphism of G-real algebraic sets if f is both equivariant (i.e. for
all g ∈G and x ∈X, f(g · x) = g · f(x)) and the restriction of a polynomial map Rn →RN.
If f is a bijective morphism of G-real algebraic sets such that f−1 is also a morphism of G-real
algebraic sets (i.e. the restriction of a polynomial map RN →Rn), we will say that f is an
isomorphism of G-real algebraic sets, or just an isomorphism when the context is clear, and
that the G-real algebraic sets X and Y are isomorphic.
Remark that, with the vocabulary we introduced in subsection 2.1, a map between G-real
algebraic sets is an isomorphism if and only if it is an equivariant polynomial isomorphism.

at the origin) is a morphism of S1-real algebraic sets.
Let X ⊂Rn be a G-real algebraic set associated to a polynomial group action α : G × X →
X ; (g, x) 7→g · x. As announced above, the action of G can be polynomially linearized: up
to an isomorphism of G-real algebraic sets, we can always suppose that the polynomial action
of G on X is given by matrices with polynomial entries in the coordinates of Rm.

a polynomial representation RN of G.

In this text, we will not only be interested in the G-real algebraic sets but also in the

Definition 3.0.11. Let S be a semialgebraic subset of Rn. We say that S is a G-semialgebraic
set if S is equipped with a group action α : G×S →S ; (g, x) 7→g ·x of G such that the map α
is the restriction of a polynomial map.

of G on the Zariski closure of S in Rn.
Therefore, a semialgebraic subset of Rn is a G-
semialgebraic set if and only if it is a G-stable subset of a G-real algebraic set of Rn.
We dedicate the last part of this section to the complexification of G-real algebraic sets:
as for real algebraic groups, considering polynomial group actions on real algebraic sets allows
to consider their complexification. In other words, polynomial group actions on real algebraic
sets are induced by complex polynomial group actions with real coefficients.
Lemma 3.0.13.
Let X ⊂Rn be a G-real algebraic set with polynomial group action α :
G×X →X ; (g, x) 7→g ·x. The complexification αC : GC ×XC →XC of α is a (polynomial)
group action of the complex algebraic group GC on the complex algebraic set XC.
Proof. Suppose that α, resp. µ, is the restriction of a polynomial map eα : Rm ×Rn →Rn, resp.
eµ : Rm × Rm →Rm. Since we have the polynomial equality eαC(·, eαC(·, ·)) = eαC(eµC(·, ·), ·) on
G×G×X, we have the same equality on the complex Zariski closure GC ×GC ×XC. Similarly,
the polynomial equality eαC(e, ·) = · on X extends on XC: these equalities are illustrations of
the functoriality of the complexification.
Examples 3.0.14.
1. The usual linear action of Ωn(R), resp. On(R), resp. SOn(R), on Rn (example 3.0.2 1)
extends to the usual linear action of Ωn(C), resp. On(C), resp. SOn(C), on Cn (see
example 2.3.4 1).

of On(R) and Ok(R) on Vk(Rn) (example 3.0.2 3). First, denote by V n

Ik}): if ϑ denotes the non-degenerate bilinear form

Finally, the complexification of a morphism of G-real algebraic sets is a morphism of GC-
complex algebraic sets :
Lemma 3.0.15. Let f : X →Y be a morphism, resp. an isomorphism, of G-real algebraic
sets. Then the complexification fC : XC →YC is a morphism, resp. an isomorphism, of GC-
complex algebraic sets (i.e. an equivariant restriction of a polynomial map, resp. an equivariant
polynomial isomorphism).
Proof. The equivariance of f implies the equivariance of fC by functoriality of the complexica-
tion (cf. section 2.3).
4
Linearly reductive real algebraic groups, invariant polyno-
mial functions and Hilbert’s finiteness theorem
In this section, we will give the proof of Hilbert’s finiteness theorem for real algebraic sets
(theorem 4.2.1), a result that we will use in the next section in order to construct a real algebro-
geometric quotient for any polynomial action of a so-called linearly reductive real algebraic
group on a real algebraic set.
Hilbert’s finiteness theorem states that, if G is a real algebraic group (in the sense of
definition 2.1.1) the R-algebra of invariant polynomial functions of any G-real algebraic set (in
the sense of definition 3.0.1) is finitely generated as soon as G is linearly reductive. In particular,
we will see that the hypotheses of polynomial group structure and polynomial group action that
we have been considering are needed here.
4.1
Linearly reductive real algebraic groups
We give below the definition of a linearly reductive real algebraic group that justifies the
employed terminology. We will thereafter give several characterizations of the notion.
Let G ⊂Rm be a real algebraic group and first remark that if V is a polynomial represen-
tation of G (definition 3.0.4), associated to a group homomorphism ρ : G →GL(V ), and if W
is a subrepresentation of V (i.e. a vector subspace of V such that for all g ∈G, ρ(g)(W) ⊂W,
23


## Page 24


so that we can consider the induced group homomorphism ρW : G →GL(W) which associates
to any g ∈G the restriction W →W of ρ(g)), then W is also a polynomial representation of G.
Definition 4.1.1. The real algebraic group G is called linearly reductive if for any polynomial
representation V of G and every subrepresentation W of V , there exists a subrepresentation
W ′ of V such that V = W ⊕W ′.
Let X ⊂Rn be a G-real algebraic set with polynomial action α : G × X →X. As in the
proof of proposition 3.0.8, α induces on the R-algebra P(X) of polynomial functions on X the
linear action of G which associates, to any g ∈G and f ∈P(X), the polynomial function
g · f : X
→
R
x
7→
f
 α(g−1, x)

.
We will then say that a polynomial function f on X is invariant if for all g ∈G, g · f = f,
and we will denote by P(X)G the set of invariant polynomial functions on X: P(X)G is an R-
subalgebra of P(X).
We will see that G is linearly reductive if and only if any G-real algebraic set has a Reynolds
operator:
Definition 4.1.2. A Reynolds operator of the G-real algebraic set X is an R-linear map
R : P(X) →P(X)G such that, for all f ∈P(X)G, R(f) = f and, for all g ∈G and f ∈P(X),
R(g · f) = R(f).
In below proposition 4.1.3, we are actually going to establish the equivalence of linearly
reductivity with other important properties of linearly reductive real algebraic groups. The
proof of the statement will be a mix of the proofs of Theorem 2.2.5 of [9] and Proposition 4.14
of [13]; in particular, it overcomes the fact that one conclusion of Schur’s Lemma over C (namely
the second point of Schur’s Lemma 1.7 in [11]) is not true in the real framework.
When V is any representation of G, denote by V G the subspace {v ∈V | ∀g ∈G, g · v = v}
of G-invariant vectors of V .
Proposition 4.1.3. The following properties are equivalent :
1. the real algebraic group G is linearly reductive,
2. for any polynomial representations V and W of G, for any surjective G-equivariant linear
map ω : V →W, we have ω
 V G
= W G,
3. for any polynomial representation V of G, for any ϕ ∈(V ∗)G \ {0}, there exists v ∈
V G \ {0} such that ϕ(v) ̸= 0,
4. for any polynomial representation V of G, there exists a subrepresentation W of V such
that V = V G ⊕W and (W ∗)G = {0},
5. any G-real algebraic set has a Reynolds operator.

of V such that V = Ker ω ⊕V ′: the map ω then restricts to a G-equivariant linear isomorphism
eω : V ′ →W and, in particular, W G = eω(V ′G) ⊂ω(V G).

4.2
Hilbert’s finiteness theorem
We have been considering linearly reductive real algebraic groups in the previous subsection
because we have in view Hilbert’s finiteness theorem. This result asserts the finite generation of
the R-algebra of invariant polynomial functions of any real algebraic set polynomially acted by
a linearly reductive real algebraic group. This algebra will then correspond to a real algebraic
set that we will call the real geometric quotient in the next section.
For the proof of Hilbert’s finiteness theorem, we follow the argumentation of [9] (Corol-
lary 2.2.11, Theorem 2.2.10 and Corollary 2.2.9).
Theorem 4.2.1. (Hilbert’s finiteness theorem) Let G be a linearly reductive real algebraic group
and let X be a G-real algebraic set. The R-algebra P(X)G is finitely generated.
Proof. Using proposition 3.0.8, we can consider an isomorphism of G-real algebraic sets i : X →
Y onto a G-algebraic subset Y of a polynomial representation RN of G. Denote by ι the induced
morphism of R-algebras P(RN) = R[x1, . . . , xN] →P(X) ; f 7→f|Y ◦i: ι is G-equivariant with
respect to the induced actions of G. For the sake of readability, denote R[x] := R[x1, . . . , xN]
in the following.
Now, let h ∈P(X)G and let f ∈R[x] such that h ◦i−1 = f|Y = f in P(Y ). Let then W
be the R-vector subspace of P(X) generated by h, and V be the subspace of R[x] generated
by the orbit {g · f | f ∈G} of f: the restriction V →W of ι is a surjective G-equivariant
linear map between polynomial representations of G. Therefore, since G is linearly reductive,
by characterization 2 of proposition 4.1.3, there exists ef ∈V G ⊂R[x]G such that ι



= h.
As a consequence, P(X)G = ι
 R[x]G
. It is thus sufficient to prove that R[x]G is a finitely
generated R-algebra to assert that so is P(X)G.
Let I be the ideal of the ring R[x] generated by the non-constant homogeneous G-invariant
polynomials of R[x]. Since the latter is a Noetherian ring, the ideal I is generated by a finite
number of polynomials and then, by definition of I, by a finite number of non-constant homo-
geneous G-invariant polynomials f1, . . . , fr. The rest of the proof will consist in showing that
the R-algebra R[x]G is generated by the polynomials f1, . . . , fr i.e. any G-invariant polynomial

4.3
Complexification
We end this part by studying the complexification of the notions and results that we addressed
in the above subsections: this will reveal crucial in the following to understand the complex-
ification of the real geometric quotient of G-real algebraic sets when G is a linearly reductive
real algebraic group.
First, the notion of linear reductivity (definition 4.1.1) has its complex counterpart for
complex algebraic groups and the complex analogs of equivalences of proposition 4.1.3 and
corollary 4.1.5 remain valid, as well as remarks 4.1.4.
Now, let G be any real algebraic group. Have in mind the following fact:
Lemma 4.3.1. Let X ⊂Rn be a real algebraic set and denote by P(XC) the C-algebra
C[x1, . . . , xn]/I(XC) of complex polynomial functions on XC. The morphism Ψ of C-algebras
which associates to any polynomial p+iq ∈C[x1, . . . , xn], with p, q ∈R[x1, . . . , xn], the element
p ⊗1 + q ⊗i of P(X) ⊗R C induces an isomorphism P(XC) →P(X) ⊗R C. In particular,
any complex polynomial function F on XC writes uniquely as fC + ihC where f and h are real
polynomial functions on X (below, we will respectively call f and h the real and imaginary
parts of F).
Proof. The morphism Ψ is surjective and, if p + iq ∈C[x1, . . . , xn] with p, q ∈R[x1, . . . , xn],
we have Ψ(p + iq) = 0 if and only if p = q = 0 in P(X) if and only if p, q ∈I(X) if and only
if p + iq ∈I(X) = I(XC).
Proposition 4.3.2. The real algebraic group G is linearly reductive if and only if the complex
algebraic group GC is linearly reductive.
Proof. For the direct implication, we use the characterization 2 of (the complex counterpart of)
proposition 4.1.3: suppose that G is linearly reductive and consider two complex polynomial
representations V and W of GC as well as a surjective GC-equivariant linear map ω : V →W.
The sets V and W are in particular R-vector spaces and ω : V →W is a R-linear map as
well; if we consider respective C-bases (v1, . . . , vn) and (w1, . . . , wN) for V and W, then the
31


## Page 32


tuples (v1, iv1, . . . , vn, ivn) and (w1, iw1, . . . , wN, iwN) are respective R-bases for V and W and
the matrix of the R-linear map ω in the latter bases is given by the real and imaginary parts
of the coefficients of the matrix of the C-map ω in the former bases. Furthermore, restricting
the considered linear actions on V and W to G, the finite-dimensional R-vector spaces V and
W are real polynomial representations of G (the entries of the matrix of the considered action
of G are given by the real and imaginary parts of the polynomial entries of the action of GC,
using the identification P(GC) ∼= P(G)⊗RC of lemma 4.3.1) and ω is a surjective G-equivariant
linear map between polynomial representations of G. Now, let w ∈W GC: w is in particular
a invariant vector of W G and then, since the real algebraic group G is linearly reductive,
there exists v ∈V G such that w = ω(v). But the equalities g · v = v, g ∈G, are given by
polynomial equalities (with coefficients in C) over the real algebraic set G and are then true
over its complexification GC. As a consequence, v ∈V GC so that w ∈ω(V GC). The complex
algebraic group GC is therefore linearly reductive.
As for the converse implication, suppose that GC is linearly reductive and consider the as-
sociated Reynolds operator RGC : P(GC) →C (cf. the complex counterpart of corollary 4.1.5).
The linear map f ∈P(G) 7→Re (RGC(fC)) ∈R (where, if z ∈C, Re(z) denotes the real part
of z) is then a Reynolds operator for the real algebraic group G, which is therefore linearly
reductive.
Examples 4.3.3.
1. The complexification of any compact real algebraic group is linearly reductive (see exam-
ple 4.1.7 2).
2. Let n ∈N\{0}. The real algebraic groups SLn(R) and Ωn(R) are linearly reductive since
their respective complexifications SLn(C) and Ωn(C) (cf. example 2.3.4 2) are linearly
reductive (use for instance [22] Chapter 7 paragraph 3.2 Theorem).
Let X ⊂Rn be a G-real algebraic set and consider the complex algebraic set XC: XC is
a GC-complex algebraic set (lemma 3.0.13). Suppose that G is linearly reductive: the complex
algebraic group GC is therefore linearly reductive. By the complex counterpart of Hilbert’s
finiteness theorem 4.2.1 (the proof is identical), the C-algebra P(XC)GC is finitely generated.
Furthermore, we can choose as generators of the latter the complexifications of the generators
of the finitely generated R-algebra P(X)G, by virtue of the next lemma. This will imply, in
the next section, that the complex geometric quotient of XC by GC is the complexification of
the real geometric quotient of X by G.
Lemma 4.3.4. The isomorphism of C-algebras P(XC) →P(X) ⊗R C of lemma 4.3.1 restricts
to an isomorphism P(XC)GC →P(X)G ⊗R C.
Proof. Let p + iq ∈P(XC)GC with p, q ∈R[x1, . . . , xn], and denote by α : G × X →X the
polynomial action of G on X.
For all g ∈G and all x ∈X, we have (p + iq)(α(g, x)) =
(p + iq)(x) i.e. p(α(g, x)) = p(x) and q(α(g, x)) = q(x). As a consequence, the real polynomial
functions p, q ∈P(X) on X are G-invariant.
32


## Page 33


On the other hand, let p, q ∈P(X)G, with p, q ∈R[x1, . . . , xn], and consider the ele-
ment p + iq of P(XC).
We have the polynomial equality (p + iq)(eαC(·, ∗)) = (p + iq)(∗)
over G × X (we use the notations of the beginning of subsection 2.3) hence over GC × XC, so
that p + iq ∈P(XC)GC.
As a consequence, if f1, . . . , fr are invariant polynomial functions on X generating the R-
algebra P(X)G, the extensions (f1)C, . . . , (fr)C generate the C-algebra P(XC)GC: if F is a GC-
invariant complex polynomial function on XC, the real and imaginary parts of F are in P(X)G
and, if f ∈P(X) satisfies f = p(f1, . . . , fr) with p ∈R[z1, . . . , zr], then fC = p
 (f1)C, . . . , (fr)C

in P(XC).
Remark 4.3.5. The property stated in lemma 4.3.4 remains true even if we do not suppose
that G is linearly reductive.
5
Real geometric quotient
Let G be a linearly reductive real algebraic group and let X ⊂Rn be a G-real algebraic set. By
Hilbert’s finiteness theorem 4.2.1, the R-algebra P(X)G is finitely generated. Consequently, as
we will see below, the inclusion P(X)G ,→P(X) corresponds, by Real Nullstellensatz (Theo-
rem 4.1.4 of [3]), to a G-invariant polynomial map from X to a real algebraic set X//G: we will
call this map the geometric quotient of X by G.
In this section, we will detail this construction of the real geometric quotient and we will
study its functoriality. Also, we will emphasize that in general, contrary to the complex case,
the real geometric quotient map is not surjective and its image is not a real algebraic set.
5.1
Construction of the real geometric quotient via the Real Nullstellensatz
As we said above, we will combine Hilbert’s finiteness theorem with Real Nullstellensatz in order
to construct the real geometric quotient of real algebraic sets polynomially acted by a linearly
reductive real algebraic group. The consequence of Real Nullstellensatz we will state below
asserts the contrafunctorial correspondence between polynomial maps between real algebraic
sets and morphisms of real finitely generated R-algebras.
Let A be a commutative ring with zero element 0A.
We say that A is real if for any
a1, . . . , ar ∈A, if a2

r = 0A then a1 = · · · = ar = 0A (remark in particular that
any subring of a real ring is itself real). Recall that an ideal I of A is called real if for any
a1, . . . , ar ∈A, if a2
1 + · · · + a2
r ∈I then a1, . . . , ar ∈I: the ideal I is real if and only if the
quotient ring A/I is real.
Theorem 5.1.1. The operation which associates to any real algebraic set Y its R-algebra P(Y )
of polynomial functions on Y gives an equivalence of categories between the category of (poly-
nomial isomorphism classes of) real algebraic sets and polynomial maps and the category of
(isomorphism classes of) real (as commutative rings) finitely generated R-algebras and mor-
phisms of R-algebras.
Proof. If Y ⊂Rn is a real algebraic set, the R-algebra P(Y ) = R[x1, . . . , xn]/I(Y ), generated
by the classes x1, . . . , xd, is real as a commutative ring: if f1, . . . , fr ∈P(Y ) satisfy f1(y)2 +

· · · + fr(y)2 = 0 for all y ∈Y , then all the functions f1, . . . , fr are the zero function on Y .
Furthermore, any polynomial map F : Y ⊂Rn →Z ⊂RN induces the morphism of R-algebras
F ∗: P(Z) →P(Y ) ; f 7→f ◦F and this operation is contrafunctorial.
On the other hand, if A is a real R-algebra generated by elements α1, . . . , αr, consider
the ideal I := {p ∈R[x1, . . . , xr] | p(α1, . . . , αr) = 0A} of R[x1, . . . , xr]: I is the kernel of the
morphism Φ : R[x1, . . . , xr] →A of R-algebras which associates to any indeterminate xk,
k ∈{1, . . . , r}, the element αk of A. Since the elements α1, . . . , αr generate the R-algebra A,
the morphism Φ is surjective and induces an isomorphism of R-algebras R[x1, . . . , xr]/I →A.
In particular, the ideal I is real so that, by the Real Nullstellensatz (Theorem 4.1.4 of [3]),
I(V (I)) = I. Therefore, if we denote by XA the real algebraic subset V (I) of Rr, the R-
algebra P(XA) is isomorphic to A. Notice that the real algebraic set XA is well-defined up
to polynomial isomorphism: if β1, . . . , βs is another system of generators for the R-algebra A
and Y is the associated real algebraic subset of Rs, the polynomial map XA →Y given by
polynomials p1, . . . , ps ∈R[x1, . . . , xr] such that, for l ∈{1, . . . , s}, βl = pl(α1, . . . , αr), is a
polynomial isomorphism whose inverse map is given by polynomials q1, . . . , qr ∈R[y1, . . . , ys]
such that, for k ∈{1, . . . , r}, αk = qk(β1, . . . , βs).
Finally, a morphism of R-algebras Ψ : A →B between real R-algebras A and B respectively
generated by elements α1, . . . , αr and β1, . . . , βs induces, in a contrafunctorial way, a polynomial
map Ψ∗: XB ⊂Rs →XA ⊂Rr given by polynomials q1, . . . , qr ∈R[y1, . . . , ys] such that, for
k ∈{1, . . . , r}, Ψ(αk) = qk(β1, . . . , βs).
Remark 5.1.2. Via the above equivalence of categories, the tensor product over R of real finitely
generated R-algebras corresponds to the Cartesian product of real algebraic sets: in particular,
the tensor product over R of two real finitely generated R-algebras A and B is a real finitely
generated R-algebra. Indeed, there exist real algebraic sets V and W such that A, resp. B,
is (isomorphic to) P(V ), resp. P(W), so that the tensor product A ⊗R B is (isomorphic to)
P(V ) ⊗R P(W) = P(V × W) which is a real finitely generated R-algebra.
Keep the notations of the beginning of the section. We apply theorem 5.1.1 and its proof to
the inclusion morphism of real finitely generated R-algebras P(X)G ,→P(X) (the real algebraic
group G is linearly reductive so that, by Hilbert’s finiteness theorem 4.2.1, the former R-
algebra is finitely generated as well): it corresponds to the restriction πX : X →XP(X)G of the
polynomial map x ∈Rn 7→(f1(x), . . . , fr(x)) ∈Rr, where f1, . . . , fr are invariant polynomial
functions on X generating the R-algebra P(X)G and
XP(X)G = V

Notice that the map πX is G-invariant: if x ∈X and g ∈G, we have
πX(g · x) = (f1(g · x), . . . , fr(g · x)) = (f1(x), . . . , fr(x)) = πX(x).
Definition 5.1.3. We denote X//G := XP(X)G and we call the polynomial map πX : X →X//G
the geometric quotient of X by G. By abuse of terminology, the real algebraic set X//G will
also be called the geometric quotient of X by G.
Remark 5.1.4. The geometric quotient is well-defined up to polynomial isomorphism.

Actually, the image of any real geometric quotient by a linearly reductive real algebraic
group is always a semialgebraic set, since the image of a polynomial map between real al-
gebraic sets is semialgebraic by Tarski-Seidenberg theorem (more precisely by its corollary
Proposition 2.2.7 of [3]).
Definition 5.1.6. Keeping the above notations, we denote by X/G the semialgebraic sub-
set πX(X) of Rr and by ϖX the restriction X →X/G of πX. The map ϖX : X →X/G will
be called the semialgebraic quotient of X by G. By abuse of terminology, the semialgebraic
set X/G will also be called the semialgebraic quotient of X by G.
Lemma 5.1.7. The Zariski closure of X/G in Rr is X//G. In particular, πX is surjective if
and only if X/G is a real algebraic set.

5.2
Functoriality of the real geometric quotient
We are going to establish the functoriality of the geometric quotient (and thus of the semial-

5.3
Geometric quotient of a real algebraic group by a linearly reductive
normal subgroup
We dedicate one part of this section to the application of the previous construction to the case
of the geometric quotient of a real algebraic group by a linearly reductive normal subgroup:
this quotient turns out to be a real algebraic group as well, as we will prove below.
Let K be a real algebraic group with polynomial binary operation µ : K × K →K and
let H be a normal real algebraic subgroup of K that we furthermore suppose to be linearly
reductive. The real algebraic group K polynomially acts on itself by left multiplication (cf.
example 3.0.2 4) and we restrict this action into the polynomial action H × K →(h, k) 7→
µ(h, k), making K into a H-real algebraic set. Since H is linearly reductive, we can consider
the geometric quotient of K by H.
Proposition 5.3.1. The geometric quotient K//H is a real algebraic group and the geometric
quotient map πK : K →K//H is a morphism of real algebraic groups.

and that the corresponding polynomial map K//H × K//H →K//H provides a real algebraic
group structure on the geometric quotient K//H.

Corollary 5.3.2. The semialgebraic quotient K/H is a semialgebraic group (definition 2.2.1)
and the semialgebraic quotient map ϖK : K →K/H is a group homomorphism.
Proof. The semialgebraic quotient K/H is a semialgebraic group as the image of the morphism
of real algebraic groups πK (see examples 2.2.2) and the semialgebraic quotient map ϖK : K →
K/H is a group homomorphism as a restriction of the latter.

is finite, H is a linearly reductive real algebraic group (cf. example 4.1.7 1). The polynomial

5.4
Complexification of the real geometric quotient
Complexify the G-real algebraic set X into the GC-complex algebraic set XC (cf. lemma 3.0.13)
and consider the inclusion morphism of C-algebras P(XC)GC ,→P(XC). Both algebras P(XC)
and P(XC)GC are finitely generated (since G is a linearly reductive real algebraic group, GC is
a linearly reductive complex algebraic group and the C-algebra P(XC)GC is finitely generated:
cf. subsection 4.3) and reduced: recall that a commutative ring A is said reduced if for any
a ∈A, the existence of a positive integer k such that ak = 0A implies that a = 0A (in particular,
any subring of a reduced ring is reduced as well).
By Hilbert’s Nullstellensatz, the operation which associates to any complex algebraic set
its algebra of complex polynomial functions gives a contrafunctorial equivalence between the
category of complex algebraic sets and polynomial maps and the category of reduced finitely
generated C-algebras and morphisms of C-algebras (see theorem 9.1.1 of the appendix: the
proof is similar to the proof of theorem 5.1.1).

of generators of P(X)G: if we keep the above considered generators f1, . . . , fr of P(X)G, then
the extensions (f1)C, . . . , (fr)C generate the C-algebra P(XC)GC.

In particular, if y ∈Z, the fiber π−1({y}) is non-empty and π is then surjective.
6
Polynomial actions of compact real algebraic groups on real
algebraic and semialgebraic sets
In the following of this text, we will focus on compact real algebraic groups and their polynomial
actions on real algebraic sets. As we have seen in example 4.1.7 2, a compact real algebraic
group is linearly reductive, so that we can consider the associated geometric and semialgebraic
quotients. But the polynomial action of a compact real algebraic group on a real algebraic set
has a further property of separation of orbits. This has many consequences, in particular the
fact that two points have the same image by the geometric quotient if and only of they have
the same orbit. We will see that the semialgebraic quotient is actually (up to homeomorphism)
the topological quotient and we will then establish several topological and geometric properties
of the semialgebraic and geometric quotients associated to a compact real algebraic group.
The first part of this section will be dedicated to compact real algebraic groups themselves.
In fact, our starting point will be to consider the orbits of the polynomial action of any compact
subgroup of a real algebraic group on a real algebraic set: due to their separation property,
those orbits are always real algebraic sets. This will imply some rigidity and stability of the
class of compact real algebraic groups: for instance, any closed subgroup of a compact real
algebraic group is a compact real algebraic group and the image of a compact real algebraic
group by a morphism of real algebraic groups is a compact real algebraic group.
In a subsequent subsection, we will establish further properties in this direction, considering
the complexification of compact (Lie) subgroups of orthogonal groups.
43

6.1
Compact subgroups of real algebraic groups
Let G ⊂Rm, m ∈N \ {0}, be a real algebraic group with binary operation µ : G × G →G and
let H be a compact subgroup of G i.e. a subgroup of G which is a closed bounded subset of Rm.
Let X ⊂Rn, n ∈N\{0}, be a G-real algebraic set with polynomial action α : G×X →X of G
on X. We are going to state the crucial fact that all the orbits of the induced action of H on X
are real algebraic sets: this will be our starting point to establish some properties of compact
real algebraic groups and their polynomial actions on real algebraic sets.
First consider the following important property of separation of orbits by an invariant
polynomial function: if x ∈X, we denote by H · x the orbit of x under the action of H on X
induced by the action of G.
Lemma 6.1.1. Let x ∈X and y ∈X \H ·x. There exists a polynomial function p on X which
is H-invariant (i.e. for all h ∈H and z ∈X, p(h · z) = p(z)) and such that p(x) ̸= p(y).

x) ∪(H · y) which takes value 1 on H · x and value −1 on H · y (we have (H · x) ∩(H · y) = ∅
and H · x and H · y are compact). By Stone-Weiertrass theorem, there exists a polynomial
of R[x1, . . . , xn] restricting into a polynomial function f on X such that for all z ∈(H·x)∪(H·y),
|F(z) −f(z)| < 1

Proposition 6.1.2. For all x ∈X, the orbit H · x := {h · x | h ∈H} of the action of H on X
is a compact real algebraic set.
44


## Page 45


Proof. Let x ∈X and first remark that the set H · x is compact, as the image of the compact
set H by the continuous map G →X ; g 7→g·x = α(g, x). We are now going to show that H ·x
is a real algebraic subset of Rn, by showing that H · x = V (I(H · x)).
Let y ∈X \H ·x (we have H ·x ⊂X). By previous lemma 6.1.1, there exists an H-invariant
polynomial function p on X such that p(x) ̸= p(y). If we denote q := p−p(x) ∈P(X), we have,
for all h ∈H, q(h · x) = 0 and q(y) ̸= 0. Therefore, there exists a polynomial Q ∈R[x1, . . . , xn]
such that Q ∈I(H ·x) and Q(y) ̸= 0, that is y /∈V (I(H ·x)). Consequently, V (I(H ·x)) ⊂H ·x
and then H · x = V (I(H · x)).
An immediate consequence of the former proposition is that any compact subgroup of a
real algebraic group is itself a real algebraic group:
Corollary 6.1.3. H is a real algebraic set and is then a real algebraic subgroup of G.
Proof. Consider the action of G on itself by left multiplication: G is a G-real algebraic set.
Therefore, by previous proposition 6.1.2, H = H · e (where e is the neutral element of G) is a
real algebraic set.
From this result, we deduce several classes of examples of compact real algebraic groups:
Examples 6.1.4.
1. Any closed (with respect to the ambient Euclidean topology) subgroup of a compact real
algebraic group is a real algebraic group.
2. Any compact semialgebraic group is a real algebraic group (since it is a subgroup of a
real algebraic group: see lemma 2.2.3).
3. Any compact subgroup of GLn(R) =
n
A ∈Rn2 | det(A) ̸= 0
o
is a real algebraic group.
Indeed, let K be a compact subgroup of GLn(R) then, considering the (polynomial)
determinant map det : Rn2 →R, det(K) is a compact subset of R. If A was a matrix of
the group K such that |det(A)| > 1, then det(K) would be an unbounded subset of R,
and if A was a matrix of K such that |det(A)| < 1, then the closed set det(K) would
contain 0, which is impossible since K ⊂GLn(R). As a consequence, det(K) ⊂{−1; 1},
that is K is a compact subgroup of the real algebraic group Ωn(R) (see example 2.1.2 1).
This also allows to show the stability of the class of compact real algebraic groups under
morphisms of real algebraic groups:
Corollary 6.1.5. The image of a compact real algebraic group by a morphism of real algebraic
groups is a compact real algebraic group.
In particular, the image of the geometric quotient of a compact real algebraic group by a nor-
mal closed subgroup (see subsection 5.3 and recall that a compact real algebraic group is linearly
reductive by example 4.1.7 2) is a compact real algebraic group (the associated semialgebraic
quotient coincides with the geometric quotient).
Proof. The image of a compact real algebraic group by a morphism of real algebraic groups,
which is in particular continuous, is a compact subgroup of a real algebraic group, hence a
compact real algebraic group by corollary 6.1.3.
45


## Page 46


Let us also prove that the irreducible components of a compact real algebraic groups coincide
with its (semialgebraic) connected components :
Proposition 6.1.6. Suppose that the real algebraic group G is compact. Then the irreducible
components of G are exactly the connected components (with respect to Euclidean topology)
of G. In particular, G is irreducible if and only if G is connected.
Proof. Because G is compact, the connected component Gc (see lemma 2.2.5) is a compact
semialgebraic group and is therefore a real algebraic group. As a consequence, by proposition
2.2.9, we have Gc = G0, hence the result since the connected components of G are the cosets
of G modulo Gc and the irreducible components of G are the cosets of G modulo G0 (see
proposition 2.1.6).
6.2
Orthogonalizability of the polynomial action of a compact real algebraic
group
Now suppose that G ⊂Rm is a compact real algebraic group and, as above, let X ⊂Rn be
a G-real algebraic set associated to a polynomial action map α : G × X →X. Before studying
the geometric quotient of X by G under this compactness hypothesis on G, let us establish
that, up to an isomorphism of G-real algebraic sets, the action of G on X can be assumed to
be given by orthogonal matrices with polynomial entries in the coordinates of Rm (recall that
we have already asserted in proposition 3.0.8 that the action of G on X can be polynomially
linearized).
If V is a real representation of a group H associated to a group homomorphism ρ : H →
GL(V ) and if V is equipped with a Euclidean inner product, we say that the considered rep-
resentation of H is orthogonal if for all h ∈H, the linear isomorphism ρ(h) is orthogonal with
respect to the considered inner product. If H is a real algebraic group, we say that an orthog-
onal representation of H is a polynomial orthogonal representation of H if it is a polynomial
representation of H (definition 3.0.4).
Proposition 6.2.1. The G-real algebraic set X is isomorphic to a G-real algebraic subset
of a polynomial orthogonal representation RN of G, where RN is equipped with its canonical
Euclidean inner product.
Proof. Thanks to proposition 3.0.8, up to an isomorphism of G-real algebraic sets, we can
suppose that the action of G on X is the restriction of an action ϱ : G × Rn →Rn of G on Rn
such that, for any g ∈G, the map ρ(g) : v ∈Rn 7→ϱ(g, v) ∈Rn is a linear automorphism given
by matrices whose entries are polynomial in g ∈Rm.

6.3
Compact subgroups of real orthogonal groups, Lie algebras and com-
plexification
Before studying the geometric and semialgebraic quotients of real algebraic sets by polynomial
actions of compact real algebraic groups, we take advantage of above proposition 6.2.2 to
assert further rigidity properties of morphisms of compact real algebraic groups. For instance,
as in the complex case but contrary to the general real case (see example 2.1.5 2), a bijective
morphism of real algebraic groups between compact real algebraic groups is an isomorphism of
real algebraic groups.
In order to show such a result, we will consider compact real algebraic subgroups of or-
thogonal groups, the associated Lie algebras, their respective complexifications as well as the

We will use this theorem to prove the announced result on morphisms of compact real
algebraic groups, through the following corollary:
Corollary 6.3.4. Let H be a compact subgroup of a real orthogonal group ON(R) and let
φ : G →H be a morphism of real algebraic groups.
1. If the linear map dInφ is injective, then Ker φC = Ker φ.
2. We have Im φC = (Im φ)C (recall that Im φ is, in this context, a real algebraic group: cf.
corollary 6.1.5).

Remark 6.3.5. Keeping the notations of above corollary 6.3.4, if dInφ is injective, then Ker φC =
Ker φ is a finite set of (real) points. Indeed, in this case, since Ker φC is a complex algebraic set
containing Ker φ, we have Ker φ ⊂(Ker φ)C ⊂Ker φC = Ker φ, hence (Ker φ)C = Ker φ. This
implies that dim Ker φ = 0, because the dimension of (Ker φ)C considered as a real algebraic
subset of R2N2 is 2 dim Ker φ (Propositions 2.2.1 and 2.2.5 of [1] and Proposition 3.1.1 of [3]).
Corollary 6.3.6. Let φ : K →H be a morphism of real algebraic groups between any compact
real algebraic groups. If φ is bijective, then φ−1 is (the restriction of) a polynomial map i.e. φ
is an isomorphism of real algebraic groups.
Proof. Using proposition 6.2.2, we can suppose that φ is a morphism of real algebraic groups
between compact subgroups of orthogonal groups.
Suppose now that φ is bijective: φ is
then a bijective morphism of Lie groups and is therefore an isomorphism of Lie groups. As
a consequence, the differential dInφ is bijective.
According to above corollary 6.3.4 1, the
morphism of complex algebraic groups φC is then injective as well, and φC is also surjective
by corollary 6.3.4 2. Consequently, by corollary 2.3.8, φ is an isomorphism of real algebraic
groups.
Corollary 6.3.7. Let φ : K →H be a morphism of real algebraic groups between compact real
algebraic groups. Then φ induces an isomorphism of real algebraic groups φ : K/Ker φ →Im φ.
Proof. First, by corollary 6.1.5, the image of φ is a compact real algebraic group and φ then
induces a morphism of real algebraic groups eφ : K →Im φ ; g 7→φ(g).
Now, consider
the (polynomial) action of the (compact) normal real algebraic subgroup Ker φ of K on K
by left multiplication, as well as the trivial action of Ker φ on Im φ.
By lemma 5.2.1, the
51


## Page 52


morphism eφ induces the polynomial map φ := eφ//Ker φ : K//Ker φ →Im φ: by corollary 6.1.5,
K//Ker φ = K/ker φ and φ is a morphism of real algebraic groups between compact real
algebraic groups. Moreover, φ is a group homomorphism since so are φ and the real algebraic
quotient πK of K by Ker φ (cf. proposition 5.3.1): for all g ∈K, we have φ (πK(g)) = φ(g).
Finally, φ is both surjective and injective, hence the result by corollary 6.3.6.
Corollary 6.3.6 can also be used to show that any continuous group homomorphism between
compact real algebraic groups is necessarily the restriction of a polynomial map:
Corollary 6.3.8. Let φ : K →H be a group homomorphism between compact real algebraic
groups which is furthermore continuous. Then φ is (the restriction of) a polynomial map.
Proof. We follow the arguments of the proof of Theorem 2.11 of Chapter 5 of [19]. Consider
the graph Γφ = {(x, y) ∈K × H | y = φ(x)} of φ: since φ is continuous, Γφ is a closed subset
of K × H. Being also a subgroup of the compact real algebraic group K × H (because φ is a
group homomorphism), Γφ is itself a compact real algebraic group (corollary 6.1.3).
Now, consider the projections π : Γφ →K ; (x, y) 7→x and ϖ : Γφ →H ; (x, y) 7→y,
and remark that π is bijective and φ = ϖ ◦π−1. But π is also a morphism of real algebraic
groups between the compact real algebraic groups Γφ and K, so that π−1 is a morphism of real
algebraic groups as well by corollary 6.3.6. Since ϖ is also a morphism of real algebraic groups,
so is the composition φ = ϖ ◦π−1. In particular, φ is the restriction of a polynomial map.
Examples 6.3.9.
1. Any continuous group homomorphism between compact subgroups of general linear groups
is polynomial in matrix entries (example 6.1.4 3).
2. Any continuous (real finite-dimensional) representation of a compact real algebraic group G
is a polynomial representation of G. Indeed, the image of a continuous representation
map of G is a compact subgroup of a general linear group and is therefore a compact real
algebraic group as well (example 6.1.4 3).
3. Any bijective continuous group homomorphism between compact real algebraic groups is
an isomorphism of real algebraic groups (combine corollary 6.3.8 and corollary 6.3.6).
Remark 6.3.10. Any compact Lie group is isomorphic, as a Lie group, to a compact real
algebraic group, and the latter is unique up to an isomorphism of real algebraic groups (see
section 2.5 of Chapter 5 of [19]).
6.4
Geometric, semialgebraic and topological quotients by the polynomial
action of a compact real algebraic group
Let G be a compact real algebraic group and let X ⊂Rn be a G-real algebraic set. Recall that,
since G is compact, G is a linearly reductive real algebraic group (example 4.1.7 2) so that
the R-algebra P(X)G is, by Hilbert’s finiteness theorem 4.2.1, finitely generated: if f1, . . . , fr
are generators of P(X)G, the geometric quotient of X by G is then the polynomial map
πX : X →X//G ; x 7→(f1(x), . . . , fr(x))
52


## Page 53


where X//G = V ({p ∈R[z1, . . . , zr] | p(f1, . . . , fr) = 0}) (see subsection 5.1).
The fact that G is compact implies the following important fact that πX separates the orbits
of the action of G on X. If x, y ∈X, recall that G · x = G · y if and only if (G · x) ∩(G · y) ̸= ∅
if and only if y ∈G · x.
Proposition 6.4.1. For all x, y ∈X, we have G · x = G · y if and only if πX(x) = πX(y). In
other words, for all x ∈X, G · x = π−1
X (πX(x)).
Proof. The direct implication is given by the invariance of the polynomial functions f1, . . . , fr.
As for the converse one, let x, y ∈X and suppose that (G · x) ∩(G · y) = ∅. In particular,
y /∈G · x and then, by lemma 6.1.1, there exists an invariant polynomial function p of P(X)G
such that p(x) ̸= p(y). As a consequence, πX(x) ̸= πX(y) since πX(x) = πX(y) if and only if
for all k ∈{1, . . . , r}, fk(x) = fk(y) if and only if for all f ∈P(X)G, f(x) = f(y) (because the
functions f1, . . . , fr generates the R-algebra P(X)G).
Remark 6.4.2. The previous result is not true for general linearly reductive real algebraic
groups. As a real counterpart of Example 2.3.1 of [9], suppose that n ≥2 and consider the
polynomial action K ×Rn →Rn ; (a, b), (x1, . . . , xn) 7→(ax1, . . . , axn) of the linearly reductive
real algebraic group K = {(a, b) ∈R2 | ab = 1} (example 4.1.7 3) on Rn.
According to
example 4.2.2 2, the real algebraic quotient map of Rn by K is constant, therefore does not
separate the infinitely many orbits of the action of K on Rn.
Corollary 6.4.3. Let A be any G-stable subset of X (i.e. for all x ∈A, for all g ∈G, g·x ∈A)
and B be any subset of X. Then πX(A∩B) = πX(A)∩πX(B) and πX(X\A) = πX(X)\πX(A).
Proof. Let x ∈A and y ∈B such that πX(x) = πX(y): by proposition 6.4.1, there exists g ∈G
such that y = g · x, so that y ∈B ∩A (because A is G-stable) and πX(y) ∈πX(A ∩B). As
for the second equality, since A is a G-stable subset of X (notice that so is X \ A), we have
πX(A) ∩πX(X \ A) = πX
 A ∩(X \ A)

= ∅.
In the following of this subsection, we will be considering a G-stable semialgebraic subset S
of X and the semialgebraic quotient of S by G, that is the restriction ϖS : S →S/G of πX
(definition 5.1.8, see also lemma 5.2.3). The previous properties will allow us to induce from
any equivariant map between two G-semialgebraic sets a well-defined map between their semi-
algebraic quotients, in a functorial way. This operation will preserves semialgebraicity as well
as continuity.
But let us first show that the semialgebraic quotient of S by G coincides with the topological
quotient of S by G. In order to prove this result, we will use some general topological properties
that we state and prove in the appendix (see section 9.3).
Proposition 6.4.4. Equip S ⊂Rn and S/G ⊂Rr with the respective topologies induced by
the usual Euclidean topology. The continuous map ϖS : S →S/G is proper, closed and open,
and ϖS is (up to left composition with an homeomorphism) the topological quotient of S by the
considered action of G.
Proof. By proposition 6.2.1 and lemma 5.2.1, we can suppose that Rn is a polynomial orthog-
onal representation of G (Rn being equipped with its canonical Euclidean inner product) and
53

that X is a G-real algebraic subset of Rn. By lemma 5.2.3, πX and ϖS are restrictions of the
geometric quotient map of Rn by the considered polynomial orthogonal action of G and all
these maps are then restrictions of the polynomial map π : Rn →Rr ; x 7→(f1(x), . . . , fr(x)),
where f1, . . . , fr are considered as generators of the R-algebra P(Rn)G = R[x1, . . . , xn]G. Let
us prove that the latter map π is proper and closed.
Consider the polynomial map ϕ : Rn →R ; x2

Remark 6.4.5. The semialgebraic quotient map of a semialgebraic set by a polynomial action of
any linearly reductive real algebraic group is not proper in general. Indeed, let n ∈N \{0} and
