Let us end this part with the general remark that the geometric (resp.
semialgebraic)
quotient of a real algebraic (resp. semialgebraic) set by a polynomial action of a compact real
algebraic group can always be computed, up to polynomial isomorphism, as a restriction of the
geometric quotient of a real affine space RN by a closed subgroup of ON(R):
Remark 6.4.11. By proposition 6.2.1 and lemma 5.2.1, the geometric quotient of X by G is
polynomially isomorphic to the geometric quotient of a G-real algebraic subset of a polyno-
mial orthogonal representation RN of G (where RN is equipped its canonical Euclidean inner
product) which is, by lemma 5.2.3, a restriction of the geometric quotient of RN by G.
Let Y be any G-real algebraic subset of RN. If ρ : RN →GL(RN) is the group homomor-
phism associated to the representation RN of G, consider the morphism of real algebraic groups
ϱ : G →ON(R) which associates to any element g of G the matrix of ρ(g) in the canonical basis
of RN: by corollary 6.1.5, the image K of ϱ is a compact real algebraic subgroup of ON(R),
with respect to which RN is a K-real algebraic set and Y is a K-real algebraic subset of RN.
Since a polynomial function of P(Y ) is invariant with respect to the action of G on Y if and
only if it is invariant with respect to the action of K, the geometric quotient Y →Y//G of Y
by G is the same map as the geometric quotient Y →Y//K of Y by K, which is a restriction
of the geometric quotient of RN by K.
6.5
Dimensions of the orbits
Keep the previous notations and let x ∈X: by proposition 6.1.2, the orbit G·x = {α(g, x), g ∈
G} of x is a compact real algebraic set. Notice furthermore that the stabilizer Gx := {g ∈G | α(g, x) = x}
of x is a (compact) real algebraic subgroup of G (since G is a real algebraic set and α is a poly-
nomial map). The dimensions of the real algebraic sets G · x, G and Gx are related by the
following equality:
Lemma 6.5.1. We have dim G · x = dim G −dim Gx.

into semialgebraic sets T1, . . . , Ts, semialgebraic sets F1, . . . , Fs and, for each i ∈{1, . . . , s}, a

7
Free polynomial actions of compact real algebraic groups
We dedicate this part to free polynomial actions of compact real algebraic groups on real
algebraic sets and their geometric and semialgebraic quotients. If G is a compact real algebraic
group and X is a G-real algebraic set on which G acts freely, i.e.
for all x ∈X, Gx =
{e} (if e denotes the neutral element of G), we will notably establish that the semialgebraic
quotient X/G satisfies an additional geometric property, namely symmetry with respect to
analytic arcs. This result will be a consequence of the fact that, because the action of G is free,
the geometric quotient map sends nonsingular points of X to nonsingular points of X//G. We
will also give a proof of the latter property, using the orbit maps associated to the action of G
on the points of X: since G acts freely on X, all orbit maps are polynomial isomorphisms.
59


## Page 60


7.1
Orbit map of a point with trivial stabilizer
For all this section, fix a compact real algebraic group G ⊂Rm with neutral element e and
let X ⊂Rn be any G-real algebraic set with polynomial action α : G × X →X of G. We
begin with the property that the orbit map associated to a point with trivial stabilizer is a
polynomial isomorphism:
Proposition 7.1.1. Let x ∈X such that Gx = {e}. The polynomial map αx : G →G ·
x ; g 7→g · x is a polynomial isomorphism (recall that the orbit G · x is a real algebraic set by
proposition 6.1.2).
Proof. Thanks to proposition 6.2.1, we can suppose that Rn is a polynomial orthogonal rep-
resentation of G (with respect to the canonical Euclidean inner product on Rn) and that X
is a G-real algebraic subset of Rn. Let ρ : Rn →GL(Rn) be the group homomorphism as-
sociated to the representation Rn of G and consider the morphism of real algebraic groups
ϱ : G →On(R) which associates to any element g of G the matrix of ρ(g) in the canonical basis
of Rn. Denote K := Im ϱ: K is a compact real algebraic group (by corollary 6.1.5) and X is
a K-real algebraic set. Furthermore, ϱ is an injective map: if g ∈G satisfies ϱ(g) = In, then
g·x = x so that g = e since Gx = {e}. As a consequence, ϱ induces a bijective morphism of real
algebraic groups G →K, which is an isomorphism of real algebraic groups by corollary 6.3.6
because G and K are both compact. Therefore, the orbit map αx : G →G · x is a polynomial
isomorphism if and only if the orbit map φx : K →K·x ; A 7→Ax is a polynomial isomorphism
(we have K · x = G · x and αx = φx ◦ϱ).
Consider the stabilizer H := (KC)x of x for the complexified action of KC on XC. First
remark that, by Proposition 21.4.3 (iii) of [26], proposition 6.5.5 and lemma 6.5.1, we have
dim H = dim KC −dim KC · x = dim K −dim K · x = dim Kx = 0
(the stabilizer Kx is reduced to In), so that H is a finite group.
Now, let A ∈H. Since K is a compact real algebraic subgroup of On(R), H is a complex
algebraic subgroup of On(C), in particular tA = A−1 ∈H. Moreover, Ax = Ax = x = x (x
has real coordinates), therefore A ∈H = (KC)x. Consequently, the positive definite Hermitian
matrix tA A is in H. On the other hand, there exist a unitary matrix U ∈Un(C) and a diagonal
matrix D with positive real diagonal coefficients such that U−1 tA AU = D. If N denotes the
order of the finite group H, we have DN = U−1  tA A
N U = In, which implies that D = IN
(because the diagonal matrix D has positive real diagonal coefficients) i.e. tA A = In i.e. A is
a unitary matrix.
Since Un(C) ∩On(C) = On(R), we deduce that H = (KC)x = Kx = {In}. The complex
orbit map KC →KC · x ; A 7→Ax, which is the complexification of φx by proposition 6.5.5,
is therefore bijective. By [26] Theorem 25.1.2 (iv), we can then assert that the KC-equivariant
polynomial map (φx)C : KC →KC · x, between KC-complex algebraic sets on which KC acts
transitively (considering the transitive action of KC on itself by left multiplication and recalling
that KC · x is a complex algebraic set by proposition 6.5.5), is a polynomial isomorphism, so
that φx is a polynomial isomorphism by lemma 2.3.7.
Remark 7.1.2. Even if the action of G on X is free, the action of GC on XC is not free in general.

7.2
Nonsingular point with trivial stabilizer
We are going to use above proposition 7.1.1 to show that the image of a nonsingular point of X
with trivial stabilizer by the geometric quotient πX is a nonsingular point of X//G. We will
also use Nakayama’s lemma (lemma 9.5.3 of the appendix) as well as the following property:
Lemma 7.2.1. Let x ∈X. If x belongs to only one irreducible component of X, then πX(x)
belongs to only one irreducible component of X//G.
Proof. Let Y1, . . . , Yl be irreducible components of X such that the irreducible algebraic sets
(G · Y1) //G, . . . , (G · Yl) //G are the irreducible components of X//G: see remark 5.1.11. Now,
suppose that πX(x) belongs to two such different components (G · Yi) //G and (G · Yj) //G
of X//G, with i, j ∈{1, . . . , l} and i ̸= j. Then, by remark 6.5.8 2, x belongs to G·Yi and G·Yj
so that there exist g, h ∈G such that x belongs to the two different irreducible components
g · Yi and h · Yj of X (we have g · Yi ̸= h · Yj since otherwise G · Yi would be equal to G · Yj and
then (G · Yi) //G would be equal to (G · Yj) //G which is not the case).
Theorem 7.2.2. Let x ∈X. Suppose that x is a nonsingular point in dimension d ∈N of X
and that Gx = {e}. Then πX(x) is a nonsingular point in dimension d −dim G of X//G.
Proof. Let Z be the unique irreducible component of X containing x: x is a nonsingular
point of Z and dim Z = d (cf. Proposition 3.3.10 of [3]). According to previous lemma 7.2.1
(see also remark 5.1.11), πX(Z)

containing πX(x).

Using previous theorem 7.2.2 and its proof, we will show that if X is irreducible and x is
a nonsingular point of X with trivial stabilizer, then the geometric quotient πX has a local
63


## Page 64


Nash section at x. This will allow us, in the next subsection, to lift analytic arcs on X/G to
analytic arcs on X if the action of G on X is free, in order to prove the arc-symmetry of the
semialgebraic quotient X/G in this case.
Let us first prove the following property given by the semialgebraic version of inverse func-
tion theorem. We refer to Definition 2.9.9 of [3] for the definition of a Nash submanifold of an
affine space and the definition of a Nash map between two such objects. If A is any subset
of Rn and y ∈A, an open semialgebraic neighborhood of y in A is the intersection of A with an
open (with respect to Euclidean topology) semialgebraic subset of Rn containing y.
Lemma 7.2.3. Let M be a Nash submanifold of Rn of dimension d ∈N, N be a Nash sub-
manifold of Rr of dimension D ∈N, f : M →N be a Nash map and y ∈M. If the differential
dyf : TyM →Tf(y)N is surjective, then there exist an open semialgebraic neighborhood U of y
in M, an open semialgebraic neighborhood V of f(y) in N and a Nash map h : V →U such
that f ◦h = IdV .
Proof. By definition, up to restrictions and left and right compositions with Nash diffeomor-
phisms between open semialgebraic sets of affine spaces sending respectively y to 0 ∈Rn

Let us state a first consequence of theorem 7.2.4, namely the further functoriality of semial-
gebraic quotient (cf. proposition 6.4.9) with respect to equivariant differentiable maps defined
on open G-semialgebraic sets on which G acts freely:
Corollary 7.2.6. Let S ⊂Rn be an open (with respect to the Euclidean topology of Rn) G-
semialgebraic set, let T ⊂RN be any G-semialgebraic set and let f : S →T be an equivariant
Ck differentiable map with k ∈N\{0}∪{∞, ω} (by a Cω differentiable map, we mean an analytic
map). Suppose that for all x ∈S, Gx = {e}. Then the semialgebraic quotient S/G ⊂Rs is
a Nash submanifold of dimension n −dim G of Rs and the map f/G : S/G →T/G ⊂Rs (see
section 6.4) is a Ck differentiable map (of Ck manifolds).
Proof. Since S is a (non-empty) open semialgebraic subset of Rn, the Zariski closure of S
in Rn is Rn (see for instance the proof of Proposition 2.8.4 of [3]: we have S
Z = V (I(S)) =
V ({0}) = Rn) so that Rn is a G-real algebraic set and S is a G-semialgebraic subset of Rn
(see lemma 3.0.12). For simplicity, denote by ϖ the semialgebraic quotient map ϖRn : Rn →
Rn/G ⊂Rr and let x ∈S: since Gx = {e}, by above theorem 7.2.4, there exist an open
semialgebraic set U of Rn containing x, an open semialgebraic neighborhood V of ϖ(x) = ϖS(x)
in Rn/G which is a Nash submanifold of dimension n−dim G of Rr and a Nash map ψ : V →U
such that ϖ ◦ψ = IdV (see also remark 7.2.5 2).
On the one hand, since S is an open semialgebraic subset of Rn and ϖ is a semialgebraic
and open map (lemma 6.4.4), V ∩ϖ(S) = V ∩S/G is an open semialgebraic subset of Rn/G
contained in V .
The set V ∩S/G is therefore an open semialgebraic subset of the Nash
submanifold V of Rr and is then itself a Nash submanifold of dimension n −dim G of Rr. But
V ∩S/G is also an open semialgebraic subset of S/G containing ϖ(x). As a consequence,
since for any point ϖ(y), y ∈S, of S/G, there exists an open semialgebraic neighborhood
of ϖ(y) in S/G which a Nash submanifold of dimension n −dim G, the set S/G is itself a Nash
submanifold of dimension n −dim G of Rr.
On the other hand, on the open semialgebraic neighborhood V ∩S/G of ϖ(x), f/G writes
as ϖT ◦f ◦ψ and f/G is therefore a Ck map on V ∩S/G (ωT is the restriction of a polynomial
map from RN to Rs and recall also, in the case k = ω, that a Nash map between Nash
submanifolds of affine spaces is analytic: cf. Proposition 8.1.8 of [3]). Consequently, the map
f/G : S/G →T/G is a Ck map.
Remark 7.2.7. Combining the previous result with proposition 6.4.9, the semialgebraic quotient
is functorial with respect to equivariant Nash maps defined on open G-semialgebraic sets on
which G acts freely.
Adapting the above proof also allows to show the functoriality of semialgebraic quotient
with respect to equivariant arc-analytic maps defined on G-semialgebraic sets on which G acts
freely: a map f : A →B between subsets of affines spaces is said arc-analytic if for any analytic
arc γ :] −1; 1[→A, the composition f ◦γ :] −1; 1[→B is analytic (see definition 9.6.26).
Proposition 7.2.8. Let S ⊂Rn and T ⊂RN be G-semialgebraic sets and let f : S →T be
an equivariant arc-analytic map. Suppose that for all x ∈S, Gx = {e}. Then f/G : S/G →
T/G ⊂Rs is an arc-analytic map.
66


## Page 67


Proof. Thanks to proposition 3.0.8 (see also proposition 6.2.1), we can suppose that Rn is a
polynomial representation of G and that S is a G-stable semialgebraic subset of Rn. Consider
the polynomial map π : Rn →Rr induced by πRn : Rn →Rn//G ⊂Rr and let γ :]−1; 1[→S/G
be an analytic arc. Let t0 ∈] −1; 1[ and let x ∈S such that π(x) = γ(t0). Since Gx = {e},
we can apply theorem 7.2.4: there exist an open semialgebraic neighborhood U of x in Rn, an
open semialgebraic neighborhood V of π(x) = γ(t0) in Rn//G which is a Nash submanifold of
Rr and a Nash map ψ : V →U such that π ◦ψ = IdV .
Since γ has values in S/G ⊂Rn//G, γ−1(S/G ∩V ) is then an open subset of ] −1; 1[
containing t0: there exists ϵ > 0 such that γ(]t0 −ϵ; t0 + ϵ[) ⊂S/G ∩V and therefore, using
proposition 6.4.1, ψ ◦γ(]t0 −ϵ; t0 + ϵ[) ⊂S ∩U (we have π ◦ψ = IdV and S is stable under the
action of G). We then write, for any t ∈]t0 −ϵ; t0 + ϵ[,
f/G ◦γ(t) = ϖT ◦f ◦ψ ◦γ(t),
which shows that f/G ◦γ is analytic at t0, because ψ is analytic, f is arc-analytic and ϖT
is the restriction of a polynomial map.
As a consequence, the map f/G : S/G →T/G is
arc-analytic.
7.3
Arc-symmetric semialgebraic quotient of a real algebraic set by a free
polynomial action of a compact real algebraic group
In this part, we will achieve one goal of this paper: we prove that the semialgebraic quotient of a
G-real algebraic set on which the compact real algebraic group G acts freely is an arc-symmetric
set of the ambient affine space.
Definition 7.3.1. Let A be a subset of Rn. We say that A is an arc-symmetric set of Rn if for
any analytic arc γ :] −1; 1[→Rn, if γ(] −1; 0[) ⊂A, then γ(] −1; 1[) ⊂A (see also definition
9.6.1).
Notice that the zero set of any analytic function form Rn to R is an arc-symmetric set of Rn
by the identity theorem for analytic functions. In particular, a real algebraic set of Rn is an
arc-symmetric set of Rn (see also example 9.6.2).
This important notion of arc-symmetric set was introduced by K. Kurdyka in [15].
In
section 9.6 of the appendix, for the sake of self-completeness, we chose to give an introduction
to arc-symmetric sets of affine spaces and real analytic manifolds, deeply inspired by [15]
and [16], including the detailed proofs of properties of semialgebraic arc-symmetric sets of
affine spaces and real projective spaces stated in these two articles, in particular the fact that
the semialgebraic arc-symmetric sets of Rn form the closed sets of a Noetherian topology on Rn
(theorem 9.6.13), called the AR topology of Rn.
Below, we are actually going to show that the semialgebraic quotient of any arc-symmetric
G-semialgebraic set on which G acts freely is an arc-symmetric set of the ambient affine space:
Theorem 7.3.2. Let S be a G-stable semialgebraic subset of X.
Suppose that S is arc-
symmetric in Rn and that for any x ∈S, Gx = {e}. Then S/G ⊂Rr is a semialgebraic
arc-symmetric subset of Rr.
67


## Page 68


Proof. Let f : X →Y be an isomorphism of G-real algebraic sets onto a G-real algebraic
subset of a polynomial orthogonal representation RN of G (proposition 6.2.1). It induces the
polynomial isomorphism f//G : X//G ⊂Rr →Y//G ⊂Rs which satisfies f//G ◦πX = πY ◦f
(lemma 5.2.1). In particular, we have f//G (S/G) = f//G (πX(S)) = πY (f(S)) = f(S)/G, so
that S/G is arc-symmetric in Rr if and only if f(S)/G is arc-symmetric in Rs by proposi-
tion 9.6.29 2: the polynomial maps f//G and f−1
//G are in particular arc-analytic. As a conse-
quence, we can suppose that Rn is a polynomial representation of G and that X is a G-real
algebraic set of Rn. In particular, S/G = πRn(S).
Consider the polynomial map π : Rn →Rr induced by πRn : Rn →Rn//G ⊂Rr. The
semialgebraic set π(S) is (Euclidean) closed in Rr since the map π is closed (see the proof
of lemma 6.4.4) and since any semialgebraic arc-symmetric set of an affine space is closed
(proposition 9.6.12). Now, let γ :] −1; 1[→Rr be an analytic arc such that γ(] −1; 0[) ⊂π(S):
since π(S) is closed and γ is continuous, we have γ(] −1; 0]) ⊂π(S). Denote w := γ(0) and
let x ∈S such that π(x) = w. Since Gx = {e}, we can apply theorem 7.2.4: there exist an open
semialgebraic neighborhood U of x in Rn, an open semialgebraic neighborhood V of w = π(x)
in Rn//G which is a Nash submanifold of Rr and a Nash map ψ : V →U such that π ◦ψ = IdV .
Remark also that, because Rn//G is a real algebraic set and then an arc-symmetric subset
of Rr, γ(] −1; 1[) ⊂Rn//G: since γ has values in Rn//G, γ−1(V ) is an open subset of ] −1; 1[
containing 0. There then exists ϵ > 0 such that γ(] −ϵ; ϵ[) ⊂V . We can therefore consider the
analytic arc eγ := ψ ◦γ :] −ϵ; ϵ[→U ⊂Rn. Because π
 eγ(] −ϵ; 0])

= γ(] −ϵ; 0]) ⊂π(S), we
have, by proposition 6.4.1 and because S is G-stable, eγ(] −ϵ; 0]) ⊂S.
But S is arc-symmetric in Rn, so that eγ(] −ϵ; ϵ[) ⊂S. As a consequence, γ(] −ϵ; ϵ[) =
π
 eγ(] −ϵ; ϵ[)

⊂π(S) and π(S) is therefore an arc-symmetric semialgebraic subset of Rr.
In particular, if the action of G on X is free, then the semialgebraic quotient X/G is
arc-symmetric in Rr.
Remark 7.3.3. In general, the arc-symmetric semialgebraic quotient of a G-real algebraic set
by a free action of G is not a real algebraic set. Consider for instance the polynomial action
of S1 on R3 given in example 5.1.5 2, as well as the compact SO2(R)-stable real algebraic set

8
AS quotients of G-AS-sets
Let G be a compact real algebraic group. In this last section, we consider arc-symmetric sets
and AS-sets of compact affine Nash manifolds, as well as actions of G on these objects which
allow to define quotients with arc-symmetric/AS structures. From these quotients, we will
construct, in a subsequent paper, further invariants with respect to equivariant continuous
maps with AS-graphs.
Arc-symmetric sets can actually be defined for any real analytic manifold (definition 9.6.35):
Definition 8.0.1. Let M be a real analytic manifold and let A be a subset of M. We say that
A is an arc-symmetric set of M if for any analytic arc γ :] −1; 1[→M, if γ(] −1; 0[) ⊂A, then
γ(] −1; 1[) ⊂A.
However, we focus on Nash manifolds: on a Nash manifold M, we can consider semialgebraic
sets and define a Noetherian topology whose closed sets are the semialgebraic arc-symmetric
subsets of M, called the AS topology of M. We further restrict our attention on compact
affine Nash manifolds, that is compact Nash manifolds that can be embedded, via a Nash
diffeomorphism, as a Nash submanifold of an affine space (notice that the real projective
spaces are compact affine Nash manifolds): on those objects, the AS-sets are exactly the finite
Boolean combinations of semialgebraic arc-symmetric subsets (we refer to the subsections 9.6.6,
9.6.7 and 9.6.8 of the appendix for the details and proofs about these notions and facts).
Definition 8.0.2. Let M be a compact affine Nash manifold and let A be a subset of M. We
say that A is an AS-set of M if A is a semialgebraic subset of M and for any analytic arc
γ :] −1; 1[→M, if γ(] −1; 0[) ⊂A, then there exists ϵ ∈]0; 1[ such that γ(]0; ϵ[) ⊂A.
8.1
Compact Nash submanifolds of real affine spaces
We begin by dealing with the case of compact Nash submanifolds of real affine spaces. Precisely,
let Rn be a polynomial representation of G with geometric quotient π : Rn →Rn//G ⊂Rr and
let M be a compact Nash submanifold of Rn.
Proposition 8.1.1. Let S be a semialgebraic arc-symmetric subset of M. Suppose that S
is globally stable under the action of G on Rn and that for any x ∈S, Gx = {e}.
Then
S/G = π(S) ⊂Rr is a semialgebraic arc-symmetric subset of Pr(R).
Proof. By proposition 9.6.63, S is an AR-closed set of Rn included in M. We then apply
theorem 7.3.2 above to assert that S/G = π(S) is an AR-closed set of Rr.
Because S is
70


## Page 71


closed in the compact space M (proposition 9.6.56), S is compact in Rn. Therefore, since π
is continuous, S/G = π(S) is compact as well, and consequently S/G is a semialgebraic arc-
symmetric subset of Pr(R) by proposition 9.6.92.
More generally, we have the following result on G-stable AS-sets of M:
Proposition 8.1.2. Let S be an AS-set of M and suppose that S is globally stable under the
action of G on Rn. Then the AS-closure of S in M is G-stable as well. Furthermore, if for
any x ∈S
AS, Gx = {e}, then S/G = π(S) ⊂Rr is an AS-set of Pr(R).
Proof. First, by lemma 9.6.76, S is an AS-set of Rn included in M. We then apply theo-
rem 7.3.5: the AR-closure S
AR of S in Rn is G-stable as well. Furthermore, S
AR is also the
AS-closure S
AS of S in M by proposition 9.6.63 (M is AR-closed in Rn). As a consequence,
if the action of G on S
AS is free, then S/G = π(S) is an AR-set of Rr by theorem 7.3.5.
Moreover, by remark 7.3.6 2, the AR-closure of S/G in Rr is π


AR
= π

AS
, and the
latter set is compact (as the image of the compact subset S
AS of Rn by the continuous map π).
Consequently, by corollary 9.6.94, S/G is an AS-set of Pr(R).
In this framework, we can prove the following important property of functoriality of the
semialgebraic quotient with respect to AS-maps between AS-sets of Nash submanifolds of affine
spaces which satisfy the hypotheses of previous proposition 8.1.2. A map f : X ⊂M →Y ⊂N
between AS-sets of compact affine Nash manifolds is called an AS-map if its graph is an AS-set
of the compact affine Nash manifold M × N (definition 9.6.82).
Theorem 8.1.3. Let N be a compact Nash submanifold of a polynomial representation Rm
of G with geometric quotient π′ : Rm →Rm//G ⊂Rs. Let X be a G-stable AS-set of M and Y

8.2
G-linearizations and G-AS-sets
In this part, using the affine case of previous subsection, we precisely emphasize the objects to
which we will be able to associate some AS-quotients well-defined up to AS-homeomorphism.
Definition 8.2.1. Let M be a compact affine Nash manifold and S be an AS-set of M equipped
with an action of G. A G-linearization of S is an equivariant homeomorphism with AS-graph
Φ : S →S′ onto a G-stable AS-set S′ of a compact Nash submanifold N of a polynomial
representation Rn of G. Such a G-linearization of S is said free if the action of G on the AS-
closure of S′ in N (which is also the AR-closure of S′ in Rn) is free. If S has a G-linearization,
we say that S is a G-AS-set. If S has a free G-linearization, we say that S is a free G-AS-set.
The above definition of a free G-linearization is justified by proposition 8.1.2 and we will be
able to consider a well-defined AS-quotient of free G-AS-sets thanks to the latter result and
theorem 8.1.3: this will be done in the next subsection. First, let us give some examples of
G-AS-sets and free G-AS-sets. Notice also that, by definition, if X is a G-AS-set, the action
G × X →X ; (g, x) 7→g · x is a continuous AS-map and is given by AS-homemorphisms, i.e.
homeomorphisms with AS-graph.
Proposition 8.2.2. Any G-real algebraic set is a G-AS-set.
Proof. Let X be a G-real algebraic set of an affine space Rn. Then X is an AS-subset of the
compact affine Nash manifold Pn(R) (X is the set-theoretic difference of the projective algebraic
set obtained by homogenizing generators of the ideal I(X) and the hyperplane at infinity of

is an AS-subset of PN(R) and the homeomorphism f has AS-graph in Pr(R) × Ps(R) (see
remark 9.6.99).

Remark 8.2.3. Any G-stable Zariski open subset U of a G-real algebraic set X ⊂Rn is a G-
AS-set as well. Indeed, being the set-theoretic difference of X and a G-stable real algebraic
subset of X, the set U is an AS-subset of Pn(R). Moreover, if Φ : X →X′ ⊂M ⊂Rd is a G-
linearization of X, Φ(U) is an AS-set of M (by theorem 9.6.85) and the restriction U →Φ(U)
of Φ is an equivariant AS-homeomorphism (see example 9.6.83 2).
Example 8.2.4. If n, k ∈N\{0}, the Stiefel manifold Vk (Rn) is an Ok(R)-AS-set on which Ok(R)
acts freely (see example 3.0.2 3) and then, since Vk (Rn) is compact (as a closed subset of
the product of k copies of the unit sphere of Rn), Vk (Rn) a free Ok(R)-AS-set. Moreover,
if the compact real algebraic group G is isomorphic to a real algebraic subgroup of Ok(R)
(proposition 6.2.2), then Vk (Rn) a free G-AS-set. Notice that the considered action of Ok(R)
restricts from a polynomial action of Ok(R) on Mn,k(R) ∼= Rnk which is given by orthogonal
matrices (the canonical scalar product on Rnk corresponds to the scalar product on Mn,k(R)
which associates to any matrices A, B of Mn,k(R) the trace of the product tAB). Notice also
that, since Vk(Rn) is a compact nonsingular real algebraic set (lemma 9.2.1), Vk(Rn) is a
compact Nash submanifold of the polynomial orthogonal representation Rnk of Ok(R) (and
of G if G is isomorphic to a real algebraic subgroup of Ok(R)).

Remark that the Cartesian product N ×· · ·×N is a compact Nash submanifold of (Rn)k and

and remark 9.6.88 1). By theorem 9.6.85, the image of Λ is an AS-set of N × · · · × N and the
restriction S →Λ(S) of Λ is an homeomorphism with AS-graph (see example 9.6.83 2).
Furthermore, if we equip the product (Rn)k with the action of G given by the permutations
induced by the product in G, (Rn)k is a polynomial representation of G (see remark 3.0.3)
and Λ is equivariant.
The Cartesian product with a fixed free G-AS-set allows to make any G-AS-set into a
“relative” free G-AS-set:
Proposition 8.2.6. Let S be a free G-AS-set and let A be any G-AS-set. Then the Cartesian
product A × S, equipped with the diagonal action G × A × S →A × S ; (g, x, y) 7→(g · x, g · y)
of G, is a free G-AS-set.
Proof. Let Φ : S →S′ be a free G-linearization of S into a compact Nash submanifold N
of a polynomial representation Rn of G and let Ψ : A →A′ be a G-linearization of A into
a compact Nash submanifold L of a polynomial representation Rl of G. First, the Cartesian
product A×S is an AS-set and the Cartesian product A′×S′ is an AS-set of the compact Nash
submanifold L × N of Rl+n (remark 9.6.71). Furthermore, the product map Ψ × Φ : A × S →
A′ × S′ ; (x, y) 7→(Φ(x), Ψ(y)) is an homeomorphism with AS-graph, since so are the maps Ψ
and Φ. Moreover, equipping the affine space Rl ×Rn with the diagonal action of G makes Rl+n
into a polynomial representation of G with respect to which the set A′ × S′ is G-stable and the
map Ψ × Φ is equivariant.
It remains to check that the action of G on the AS-closure of A′ × S′ in L × N (i.e. the
AR-closure of A′ ×S′ in Rl+n) is free. But, according to corollary 9.6.34, we have A′ × S′AR =
A′AR × S′AR and, if g ∈G, x ∈A′AR and y ∈S′AR satisfy (x, y) = (g · x, g · y), then in
particular g · y = y, so that g = e (the action of G on S′AR is free by hypothesis).
As a consequence, the product map Ψ × Φ is a free G-linearization of A × S and A × S is
a free G-AS-set.
Example 8.2.7. If A is any G-AS-set, G is isomorphic to a real algebraic subgroup of Ok(R)
and n ∈N\{0}, then the Cartesian product A×Vk (Rn) is a free G-AS-set (see example 8.2.4).
Let us also give the following remark:

8.3
AS-quotient of free G-AS sets
We are now going to use proposition 8.1.2 and theorem 8.1.3 to define an AS-quotient for any
free G-AS-set, which will be well-defined up to homeomorphism with AS-graph. Let S be a
free G-AS-set.
Proposition 8.3.1. Let Φ : S →S′ and Ψ : S →S′′ be free G-linearizations of S. The map
 Ψ ◦Φ−1

remark 9.6.84 1 and
corollary 9.6.87) between AS-sets satisfying the hypotheses of theorem 8.1.3. Therefore, by the
latter result and by proposition 8.1.2 as well as proposition 6.4.9, the induced map
 Ψ ◦Φ−1
/G :
S′/G →S′′/G is an AS-homeomorphism.
Definition 8.3.2.
If Φ : S →S′ is a free G-linearization of the free G-AS-set S, we denote
by S/G the AS-set S′/G and we define the AS-quotient ϱS : S →S/G of S by G to be the
composition of Φ with the semialgebraic quotient ϖS′ : S′ →S′/G of S′ by G. By abuse of
terminology, the AS-set S/G will also be called the AS-quotient of S by G.
The AS-quotient ϱS : S →S/G is a continuous AS-map which is, according to above propo-
sition 8.3.1, well-defined up to AS-homeomorphism. We can furthermore show the functoriality
of this AS-quotient with respect to continuous AS-maps:
Theorem 8.3.3. Let T be a free G-AS-set and let f : S →T be a continuous map with AS-
graph. The map f induces, in a functorial way, a continuous AS-map f/G : S/G →T/G such
that f/G ◦ϱS = ϱT ◦f.
Proof. Let Φ : S →S′, resp.
Ψ : T →T ′, be a free G-linearization of S, resp.

(by proposition 6.4.9 and using the fact that the maps Φ and Ψ are homeomorphisms).
The AS-quotient of free G-AS-sets also preserves closed inclusions in the following sense:
Proposition 8.3.5. Let M be a compact affine Nash manifold such that S is an AS-set of M
and let T be an AS-set of M such that T is a G-stable subset of S. Then T is a free G-AS-set
and, if T is closed in S, T/G is a closed subset of S/G.
76


## Page 77


Proof. Let Φ : S →S′ be a free G-linearization of S into a compact Nash submanifold N of
a polynomial representation Rn of G. Then the restriction T →Φ(T) is a free G-linearization
of T.
Indeed, T ′ := Φ(T) is an AS-set of N by theorem 9.6.85 and the latter map is an
equivariant AS-homeomorphism (cf. example 9.6.83 2). Moreover, the AS-closure of T ′ in N
is included in the AS-closure of S′ in N and therefore the action of G on T ′ is free. Finally,
notice that, if T is closed in S, since Φ is an homeomorphism, T ′ is closed in S′, so that
T/G = T ′/G = ϖS′(T ′) is a closed subset of S′/G = S/G, because the map ϖS′ : S′ →S′/G
is closed (proposition 6.4.4).
Remarks 8.3.6.
1. With the above notations, if T is open in S, then T/G is an open subset of S/G.
2. The AS-quotient of T by G is (up to AS-homeomorphism) the restriction T →T/G of
the AS-quotient ϱS : S →S/G of S.

The relative AS-quotient also preserves closed inclusions:
Proposition 8.4.3. Let M be a compact affine Nash manifold such that A is an AS-set of M
and let B be an AS-set of M such that B is a G-stable subset of A. Then B is a G-AS-set
and, if B is closed in A, BS/G is a closed subset of AS/G.
Proof. First, let Ψ : A →A′ be a G-linearization of A into a compact Nash submanifold L
of a polynomial representation Rl of G. Then the restriction B →Ψ(B) is a G-linearization
of B since B′ := Ψ(B) is an AS-set of L by theorem 9.6.85 and the latter map is then an
equivariant AS-homeomorphism. Moreover, if B is closed in A, then B ×S is a G-stable closed
subset of A × S and we can use proposition 8.3.5 to assert that BS/G is closed in AS/G.
In the same way (see also proposition 8.3.5), we can show that if T is an AS-set which is
a G-stable closed subset of S, then AT /G is a closed subset of AS/G.
Example 8.4.4. Suppose that G is isomorphic to a real algebraic subgroup of Ok(R) and consider
the free G-AS-sets Vk (Rn), n ∈N \ {0} (example 8.2.4). For any n ∈N \ {0}, we have an

9
Appendix
In this appendix, we gather some more or less classical properties that are used in the present
document, together with their proofs, in order for this text to be almost self-contained.
9.1
Around Hilbert’s Nullstellensatz
Hilbert’s Nullstellensatz (cf. for instance Theorem 1.2 of [5]) asserts that, when I is an ideal
of the polynomial ring C[x1, . . . , xn], then I (V(I)) = I if and only if I is radical (if B is any
commutative ring and J is an ideal of B, J is said radical if for any b ∈B, the existence of a
positive integer k such that bk ∈J implies that b ∈J). We will give two consequences of this
result. First, let us state the complex counterpart of theorem 5.1.1:
Theorem 9.1.1. The operation which associates to any complex algebraic set Z its C-algebra
of polynomial functions on Z gives an equivalence of categories between the category of (poly-
nomial isomorphism classes of) complex algebraic sets and polynomial maps and the category
of (isomorphism classes of) reduced (as commutative rings) finitely generated C-algebras and
morphisms of C-algebras (recall that a commutative ring A is said reduced if for any a ∈A,
the existence of a positive integer k such that ak = 0A implies that a = 0A).
78


## Page 79


Proof. The proof is analog to the proof of theorem 5.1.1, using the fact that, if f is a polynomial
function on a complex algebraic set Z and k is a positive integer such that fk(z) = 0 for
all z ∈Z, then f is the zero function on Z. Furthermore, if B is any commutative ring and J
is an ideal of B, J is radical if and only if the quotient ring B/J is reduced and we can apply
Hilbert’s Nullstellensatz in the second part of the proof.
In the previous statement, we can replace the word “polynomial” with the word “regular”
since any regular map on a complex algebraic set (a regular map on a complex algebraic set Z
is a map Z →Cn whose any coordinate function is of the form z ∈Z 7→p(z)
q(z) ∈C where p
and q are polynomial functions on Z such that for all z ∈Z, q(z) ̸= 0) is the restriction of a
polynomial map: this is the second consequence of Hilbert’s Nullstellensatz that we state here.
Theorem 9.1.2. Let f : Z →C be a regular function on a complex algebraic set Z of Cm.
Then f is a polynomial function on Z.
Proof. Let F1, . . . , Fk, P, Q ∈C[x1, . . . , xm] such that Z = V(F1, . . . , Fk) and, for any z ∈Z,
Q(z) ̸= 0 and f(z) = P(z)
Q(z). Denote by I the ideal of C[x1, . . . , xm] generated by the polynomials
F1, . . . , Fk, Q: we have V(I) = ∅and then I (V(I)) = C[x1, . . . , xm]. On the other hand, by
Hilbert’s Nullstellensatz (see for instance Theorem 1.2 of [5]), I (V(I)) is the radical of I, so
that the constant polynomial 1 belongs to the latter and therefore to I. As a consequence,
there exist polynomials R1, . . . , Rk, S ∈C[x1, . . . , xm] such that 1 = SQ + Pk
l=1 RlFl and then,
for any z ∈Z, 1 = S(z)Q(z) so that we can write
f(z) = 1 × f(z) = S(z)Q(z)P(z)
Q(z) = S(z)Q(z).

Lemma 9.2.2. Suppose that k < n. Then the complex algebraic set V n
k (C) of Cnk is irreducible.
Proof. The complex algebraic group On(C), resp. SOn(C), polynomially acts on V n
k (C) by left

determinant 1, on Cn (see example 3.0.14 2 for the definition of the bilinear form ϑ). Since the
bilinear symmetric form ϑ is non-degenerate, Witt’s theorem can be used to assert that the
polynomial action of SOn(C) on V n
k (C) is transitive (if A and B are any two k-tuples of V n
k (C),
the linear isomorphism from Span(A) to Span(B) that sends A onto B is an isometry which
extends into an isometry of Cn which can be supposed to be of determinant 1 since k < n).

k (C) is irreducible, as the image of SOn(C) by
the surjective polynomial orbit map O ∈SOn(C) 7→OA ∈V n
k (C), where A ∈V n
k (C).
If k < n, the real algebraic set Vk (Rn) of Rnk is irreducible as well: we can adapt the
previous proof considering the canonical scalar product on Rn or argue that V n
k (C) is the
complexification of Vk (Rn) (the latter fact uses what we showed above: see example 3.0.14 2).
9.3
Continuous, proper, closed, open maps and locally compact sets
Let us now assert and prove the following topological properties that we used in the proof of
proposition 6.4.4:
80


## Page 81


Lemma 9.3.1. Let φ : A →B be a continuous map between Hausdorff topological spaces A
and B.
1. If there exists a continuous map ψ : B →C between Hausdorff topological spaces such
that ψ ◦φ is proper, then φ is proper.
2. Let B′ be a subset of B. If φ is proper, then the restriction φ−1(B′) →B′ of φ is proper
as well.
3. If φ is proper and B is locally compact, then φ is a closed map.
4. Let A′ be a subset of A. If the map φ is closed, resp. open, and if for any closed, resp.
open, subset D of A, φ(D ∩A′) = φ(D) ∩φ(A′), then the restriction A′ →φ(A′) of φ is
closed, resp. open, as well.
Proof.
1. Let ψ : B →C be a continuous map between Hausdorff topological spaces such
that ψ ◦φ is proper and let K be a compact subset of B. We have φ−1(K) ⊂(ψ ◦
φ)−1(ψ(K)). But φ−1(K) is a closed subset of A (φ is continuous and K is closed in B
since B is Hausdorff) and (ψ ◦φ)−1(ψ(K)) is compact, since ψ is continuous and ψ ◦φ
is proper. As a consequence, φ−1(K) is compact.
2. Suppose that φ is proper. If K is a compact subset of B′, then K is a compact subset
of B and, since φ is proper, φ−1(K) is a compact subset of A included in φ−1(B′) and
then a compact subset of φ−1(B′).
3. Suppose that φ is proper and B is locally compact, and let D be a closed subset of A:
let us show that φ(D) = φ(D). Let y ∈φ(D) and let U, resp. K be an open, resp.
a compact, subset of B such that y ∈U ⊂K (B is locally compact). Remark that
y ∈K ∩φ(D): if V is an open neighborhood of y, so is the intersection U ∩V and
then (U ∩V ) ∩φ(D) ̸= ∅, in particular V ∩(K ∩φ(D)) ̸= ∅. Denote L := φ−1(K): L
is compact since φ is proper and then L ∩D is compact as well (since D is closed),
so that φ(L ∩D) is also compact (φ is continuous), in particular closed in B (B is
Hausdorff). On the other hand, φ(L ∩D) = φ
 φ−1(K) ∩D

= K ∩φ(D), and then
y ∈K ∩φ(D) = φ(L ∩D) = φ(L ∩D) ⊂φ(D).
4. Suppose that φ is closed and that for any closed subset D of A, φ(D∩A′) = φ(D)∩φ(A′).
Consider a closed subset F ∩A′ of A′, where F is a closed subset of A, then φ(F ∩A′) =
φ(F) ∩φ(A′) is a closed subset of φ(A′), since φ(F) is closed in B because the map φ
is closed. Finally, notice that the same arguments work if we replace the word “closed”
with “open”.
Let us also prove the following statement about locally compact subsets of a locally compact
Hausdorff space:
Lemma 9.3.2. Let A be a locally compact Hausdorff space.
81


## Page 82


1. If K is any compact subset of A and x is an element of A \ K, then there exist disjoint
open subsets U and V of A such that x ∈U and K ⊂V .
2. If x is any element of A and U is any open neighborhood of x in A, then there exists an
open neighborhood V of x such that the closure V of V in A is compact and contained
in U.
3. Any open or closed subset of A is locally compact.
4. If D is any subset of A, then D is locally compact if and only D is locally closed.
Proof.
1. For all y ∈K, consider an open neighborhood Uy of x in A and an open neighbor-
hood Vy of y in A such that Uy ∩Vy ̸= ∅(A is Hausdorff). The open sets Vy, y ∈K, cover
the compact set K, so that there exists y1, . . . , yn ∈K such that K ⊂Sn
i=1 Vyi. If we then
set U := Tn
i=1 Uyi and V := Sn
i=1 Vyi, U and V are both open in A and satisfy U ∩V = ∅.
2. Since A is locally compact, there exist an open subset W and a compact subset K of A
such that x ∈W ⊂K. Denote by V the open set U ∩W, which is contained in K, and
remark that K \V is then a closed subset of the compact set K. The set K \V is therefore
compact as well and, since x /∈K \ V , by the first item, we can find open subsets U1, U2
of A such that x ∈U1, K \ V ⊂U2 and U1 ∩U2 = ∅. If we finally set V0 := V ∩U1 ⊂K,
we have V0 ⊂K and
V0 ∩U2 ⊂U1 ∩U2 ⊂U1 ∩U2 = ∅
(we have U1 ∩U2 ⊂U1 ∩U2 since U2 is open) so that V0 ⊂V .
Therefore, we have
x ∈V0 ⊂V0 ⊂V ⊂U and V0, being a closed subset of K, is compact.
3. Let C be a subset of A. If C is open and x ∈C, there exist, by the previous statement, an
open neighborhood V of x such that V ⊂V ⊂C and V is compact, so that C is locally
compact. If C is closed and x ∈C, let U be an open subset and K be a compact subset
of the locally compact space A such that x ∈U ⊂K: we have x ∈U ∩C ⊂K ∩C ⊂C,
where U ∩C is an open subset of C and K ∩C is a compact subset of C (C is closed),
so that C is locally compact in this case as well.
4. Let us first recall that D is a locally closed subset of A if D is the intersection of an open
set U and a closed set F of A, and that D is locally closed in A if and only if D is the
intersection of an open set U of A and the closure D of D in A, if and only if D is an
open subset of D.
Now, suppose that D is locally compact and let x ∈D: there exists an open subset U
and a compact subset K of A such that x ∈U ∩D ⊂K ⊂D. We then have x ∈U ∩D ⊂
U ∩D ⊂K ⊂D (we have U ∩D ⊂U ∩D since U is open and U ∩D ⊂K since the
compact set K is closed in A because A is Hausdorff). As a consequence, D is open in D
i.e. D is a locally closed subset of A.
As for the converse implication, since any intersection of two locally compact subsets of A
is locally compact, if D is locally closed i.e. if D is the intersection of an open and a
closed subset of A, then D is locally compact by the third item.

Lemma 9.5.3. Let R be a commutative ring, I be a proper ideal of R and M be a finitely
generated R-module, and consider the submodule IM of M.

1. Suppose that IM = M. Then there exists b ∈1 + I such that bM = {0M}.
2. Let N be a submodule of M such that M = IM + N. Then there exists b ∈1 + I such
that bM ⊂N.
3. Let ϕ : N →M be a morphism of R-modules such that the composition ϕ : N →M/IM
of ϕ with the quotient morphism M →M/IM ; x 7→x is surjective. Then there exists
b ∈1 + I such that the morphism of Rb-modules ϕb : Nb →Mb which associates to any

the R-module M/IM.
Then there exists b ∈1 + I such that x1, . . . , xr generate the
Rb-module Mb.
Proof.
1. This first statement is standard Nakayama’s Lemma: see for instance Proposition
2.6.7 of [26] for a proof.
2. The equality M = IM + N implies the equality I(M/N) = M/N and we can therefore

such that b(M/N) =

exists y ∈N such that bx = ϕ(y) and then

9.6
Arc-symmetric sets and AS-sets
In the final part of this appendix, we chose to consider notions, properties and results of the
papers [15] and [16] around the notion of arc-symmetric set which was first introduced by K.
Kurdyka in [15]. We will principally follow the outline of [16] and give the detailed proofs of
most statements.
9.6.1
Arc-symmetric sets of real affine spaces
We begin with the notion of arc-symmetric subset of a real affine space. Let n ∈N \ {0}.
Definition 9.6.1. Let S be a subset of Rn. We say that S is an arc-symmetric set of Rn if
for any analytic arc γ :] −1; 1[→Rn, if γ(] −1; 0[) ⊂S, then γ(] −1; 1[) ⊂S.
88


## Page 89


Example 9.6.2. Let f : Rn →R be an analytic function and denote X := f−1 ({0}). Then X is
arc-symmetric in Rn. Indeed, let γ :] −1; 1[→Rn be an analytic arc such that γ(] −1; 0[) ⊂X,
i.e. such that the analytic function f ◦γ :] −1; 1[→R vanishes on ] −1; 0[: by the identity
theorem (or equivalently the principle of isolated zeros) for analytic functions, the function f ◦γ
then vanishes on the entire interval ] −1; 1[ meaning that γ(] −1; 1[) ⊂X. In particular, any
real algebraic subset of Rn is arc-symmetric in Rn (recall that any real algebraic set can be
written as the vanishing locus of a single polynomial, using sums of squares).
Arc-symmetric sets of Rn can also be characterized as follows:
Lemma 9.6.3. Let S be a subset of Rn. The following properties are equivalent :
1. S is arc-symmetric in Rn,
2. for any analytic arc γ :]ϵ; ϵ′[→Rn, if there exists ϵ′′ ∈]ϵ; ϵ′[ such that γ(]ϵ; ϵ′′[) ⊂S or
γ(]ϵ′′; ϵ′[) ⊂S, then γ(]ϵ; ϵ′[) ⊂S,
3. for any analytic arc γ :] −1; 1[→Rn, if the interior of γ−1(S) in ] −1; 1[ is non-empty,
then γ(] −1; 1[) ⊂S,
4. for any analytic arc γ :] −1; 1[→Rn, if γ(] −1; 0[) ⊂S, then there exists ϵ ∈]0; 1[ such
that γ(] −1; ϵ[) ⊂S.
Proof. 1) ⇒2) : Suppose that S is arc-symmetric in Rn and let γ :]ϵ; ϵ′[→Rn be an analytic arc.
Let ϵ′′ ∈]ϵ; ϵ′[ and denote by φ the restriction ]−1; 1[→]ϵ; ϵ′[ of the increasing affine map R →R
which sends ] −1; 1[ onto ]ϵ; ϵ′[, as well as ξ := φ−1(ϵ′′) ∈] −1; 1[. Now, let ψ be the increasing
analytic diffeomorphism ] −1; 1[→] −1; 1[ ; x 7→

so that γ(]ϵ; ϵ′[) = eγ(] −1; 1[) ⊂S since S has been supposed arc-symmetric. If γ(]ϵ′′; ϵ′[) ⊂S,

is then an analytic arc ] −1; 1[→Rn such that γ(] −1; 0[) = γ ◦φ ◦ψ(]0; 1[) = γ(]ϵ′′; ϵ′[) ⊂S so
that γ(]ϵ′′; ϵ′[) = γ(] −1; 1[) ⊂S.
2) ⇒3) : Suppose that S satisfies property 2) and let γ :] −1; 1[→Rn be an analytic

3) ⇒4) : Suppose that S satisfies 3) and let γ :] −1; 1[→Rn be an analytic arc such
that γ(] −1; 0[) ⊂S i.e. ] −1; 0[⊂γ−1(S). In particular, the interior of γ−1(S) in ] −1; 1[ is
non-empty and then, by 3), γ(] −1; 1[) ⊂S.
4) ⇒1) : Suppose that S satisfies last property 4) and let γ :] −1; 1[→Rn be an analytic
arc such that γ(] −1; 0[) ⊂S. By 4), there then exists ϵ ∈]0; 1[ such that γ(] −1; ϵ[) ⊂S. Let ξ
denote the supremum of the set {ε ∈]0; 1[ | γ(] −1; ε[) ⊂S} in R: in particular, γ(]−1; ξ[) ⊂S.
Assume by absurd that ξ < 1 and consider the analytic arc γ ◦ψ :] −1; 1[→Rn where ψ is the
above increasing analytic diffeomorphism ] −1; 1[→] −1; 1[ ; x 7→x+ξ
ξx+1: we have ψ(0) = ξ and
γ◦ψ(]−1; 0[) = γ(]−1; ξ[) ⊂S, so that, by 4), there exists η ∈]0; 1[ such that γ◦ψ(]−1; η[) ⊂S,

i.e. γ(] −1; ψ(η)[) ⊂S. But ψ(η) > ξ (ψ is an increasing function) hence a contradiction. As
a consequence, ξ = 1 and γ(] −1; 1[) ⊂S.
Let us use the previous characterizations to highlight some classes of examples of arc-
symmetric sets of real affine spaces:
Examples 9.6.4.
1. Let X ⊂Rn be an analytic subset of Rn i.e.
for any x ∈X, there exist an open
neighborhood U of x in Rn and an analytic function f : U →R such that X ∩U = f−1(0).
If X is closed in Rn (with respect to Euclidean topology), then X is arc-symmetric
in Rn. Indeed, suppose that X is closed and let γ :] −1; 1[→Rn be an analytic arc
such that γ(] −1; 0[) ⊂X. Since X is closed and γ is continuous, the point x := γ(0)
also belongs to X and we can then consider an open neighborhood U of x in Rn and
an analytic function f : U →R such that X ∩U = f−1(0).
The set γ−1(U) being
an open neighborhood of 0 in ] −1; 1[, there exists ϵ > 0 such that ] −ϵ; ϵ[⊂γ−1(U)
i.e. γ(] −ϵ; ϵ[) ⊂U. Because γ(] −1; 0]) ⊂X, we have γ(] −ϵ; 0]) ⊂X ∩U = f−1(0)
i.e. the analytic function f ◦γ|]−ϵ;ϵ[ :] −ϵ; ϵ[→R vanishes on ] −ϵ; 0], and therefore on
the entire interval ] −ϵ; ϵ[ (by the identity theorem for analytic functions) meaning that
γ(] −ϵ; ϵ[) ⊂X ∩U. As a consequence, γ(] −1; ϵ[) ⊂X and X is arc-symmetric by
characterization 4 of previous lemma 9.6.3.
2. The connected components (with respect to Euclidean topology) of real algebraic sets
of Rn are closed in Rn and analytic, hence arc-symmetric in Rn by the previous example.
Indeed, let Y = h−1(0) be a real algebraic subset of Rn, with h a polynomial function
on Rn, and let C be a connected component of Y (notice that C is closed in Y which is
closed in Rn, hence C is closed in Rn). Let U be an open subset of Rn containing C such
that U ∩(Y \ C) = ∅(C and Y \ C are disjoint closed subsets of Rn since Y is closed
in Rn): we have C = h−1
|U (0) and h|U : U →R is an analytic function, so that C is an
analytic subset of Rn.
3. Closed analytic submanifolds of Rn are also instances of closed analytic subsets of Rn,
hence of arc-symmetric sets of Rn. Indeed, let M be an analytic submanifold of Rn of
dimension d ∈N and let x ∈M: there exists an analytic diffeomorphism ϕ from an open
neighborhood U of x in Rn onto an open neighborhood V of the origin in Rn such that
ϕ(x) = 0 and
ϕ(M ∩U) = {(x1, . . . , xn) ∈V | xd+1 = . . . = xn = 0} .
If, for i ∈{d + 1, . . . , n}, we denote by πi : Rn →R the projection on the ith co-
ordinate, we have M ∩U = f−1(0) where f is the analytic function U →R ; y 7→
 π2
d+1 + · · · + π2
n

(ϕ(y)): M is therefore an analytic subset of Rn.
All the above arc-symmetric sets are analytic sets but the class of arc-symmetric sets is
actually wider than the class of analytic sets: we consider below the example given in [15]
(Exemple 1.2 2)) and [16] (Example 2.10) of a closed semialgebraic arc-symmetric set which is
not an analytic set. As in the latter references, consider also the following statement:
90


## Page 91


Lemma 9.6.5. Let S be a (non-empty) subset of Rn. Suppose that S is homogeneous, i.e. for
all x ∈S and t ∈R, tx ∈S (in particular, the origin of Rn belongs to S), and analytic at
the origin of Rn, i.e. there exist an open neighborhood U of the origin in Rn and an analytic
function f : U →R such that S ∩U = f−1(0). Then S is an algebraic subset of Rn.
Proof. We can assume that, on U, f is a (convergent) infinite sum of homogeneous polynomial

Example 9.6.6. Consider the real algebraic Cartan umbrella X :=

(x, y, z) ∈R3 | z(x2 + y2) = x3

First, let γ :] −1; 1[→R3 be a non-constant analytic arc such that γ(] −1; 0[) ⊂S ⊂X:
since X is a real algebraic subset of R3, we have γ(] −1; 1[) ⊂X (example 9.6.4 2). Suppose
by absurd that γ(] −1; 1[) is not entirely contained in S i.e. γ−1(X \ S) ̸= ∅. The set X \ S =

(x, y, z) ∈R3 | x = y = 0, z ̸= 0
	
is an open subset of X and then, since γ(] −1; 1[) ⊂X,

]−1; 1[. But X \S is the set-theoritic difference of the handle T :=

of the Cartan umbrella X and the origin of R3, so that I ⊂γ−1(X \ S) ⊂γ−1(T). The interior
of γ−1(T) is therefore non-empty: since T is an algebraic set, we have γ(] −1; 1[) ⊂T. As a
consequence, γ(] −1; 0[) ⊂S ∩T = {(0, 0, 0)} i.e. γ is constant equal to (0, 0, 0) on ] −1; 0[,
hence on ] −1; 1[ (since γ is analytic), which is in contradiction with our hypothesis on γ.
Now, let us prove that the set S is not analytic at (0, 0, 0), using previous lemma 9.6.5.
Suppose by absurd that S is analytic at the origin of R3 and remark that S is an homogeneous
set: by lemma 9.6.5, S is then an algebraic subset of R3. On the other hand, S writes as the
disjoint union of {(0, 0, 0)} and the graph of the regular map φ : R2 \ {(0, 0)} →; (x, y) 7→
x3
x2+y2 . As a consequence, dim S = dim R2 \ {(0, 0)} = 2 (see for instance Proposition 2.8.5 (i)

and Theorem 2.8.8 of [3]) and S is then a proper algebraic subset of the irreducible algebraic
set X (the polynomial z(x2 + y2) −x3 of R[x, y, z] is irreducible and its sign changes on R3:
see Theorem 4.5.1 of [3]) such that dim S = dim X = 2, hence a contradiction (see for instance
Proposition 2.2.5 e) of [1]).
Remark 9.6.7. To conclude this part on examples of arc-symmetric sets of real affine spaces,
let us notice that the connected components of an arc-symmetric set of Rn are arc-symmetric
as well. Indeed, let S ⊂Rn be an arc-symmetric set of Rn and let (Si)i∈I be the family of
connected components of S. Let j ∈I and let γ :] −1; 1[→Rn be an analytic arc such that
γ(] −1; 0[) ⊂Sj. Then, since Sj ⊂S and S is arc-symmetric, we have γ(] −1; 1[) ⊂S and
γ(] −1; 1[) = F
i∈I γ(] −1; 1[) ∩Si. But, for all i ∈I, γ(] −1; 1[) ∩Si is closed and open in
γ(] −1; 1[) and γ(] −1; 1[) is connected so that, necessarily, γ(] −1; 1[) ⊂Sj.
Before stating a first stability property of arc-symmetric sets of real affine spaces, let us
mention that Lemma 2.1 of [16] (see also Lemme 0.1 of [15]) allows to consider only injective
analytic arcs in definition 9.6.1. Firstly, notice that the arguments of the proof of lemma 9.6.3
are still valid if we consider only injective analytic arcs, so that the considered equivalences of
lemma 9.6.3 remain true if we replace the words “analytic arc” with “injective analytic arc”.
Lemma 9.6.8. A subset S of Rn is arc-symmetric if and only if for any injective analytic arc
γ :] −1; 1[→Rn, if γ(] −1; 0[) ⊂S, then γ(] −1; 1[) ⊂S.
Proof. Suppose that S satisfies the latter and let γ :] −1; 1[→Rn be any analytic arc such
that γ(] −1; 0[) ⊂S. By Lemma 2.1 of [16], there exist open sub-intervals I and J of ] −1; 1[
containing 0, an analytic diffeomorphism ϕ : I →J sending 0 to 0, a positive integer k and
an injective analytic arc η : J →Rn such that, for any t ∈J, tk ∈J and γ(ϕ−1(t)) = η(tk).
Therefore, if δ denotes the infimum of I and ψ denotes the map t ∈J 7→tk ∈J, we have
η(ψ ◦ϕ(]δ; 0[)) = γ(]δ; 0[) ⊂S.
But ψ ◦ϕ(]δ; 0[) is a non-empty open sub-interval of J and η is an injective analytic arc, so
that, by hypothesis on S, we have η(J) ⊂S. As a consequence,
γ(I) = γ
 ϕ−1(J)

= η ◦ψ(J) ⊂η(J) ⊂S
and then the interior of γ−1(S) in ] −1; 1[ is non-empty: S is thus arc-symmetric in Rn by the
third characterization of lemma 9.6.3.
It is also proven in [15] that, in definition 9.6.1, if S is a semialgebraic subset of Rn, we can
consider only Nash (i.e. analytic and semialgebraic) arcs ] −1; 1[→Rn (see Remarque 1.1.1
and Lemme 2.9 of [15]). Remark that, if S is a semialgebraic subset of Rn, the equivalences
of lemma 9.6.3 remain valid if we consider only Nash arcs, since the analytic diffeomorphisms
considered in the proof have semialgebraic graph. Moreover, in definition 9.6.1, we can consider
only injective Nash arcs:
Lemma 9.6.9. Let S be a semialgebraic subset of Rn. The set S is arc-symmetric in Rn if
and only if for any injective Nash arc γ :] −1; 1[→Rn, if γ(] −1; 0[) ⊂S, then γ(] −1; 1[) ⊂S.

(see subsection 8.1 and Proposition 8.1.8 of [3]). But, since ηi : J →R is an analytic function,
by the identity theorem for analytic functions, the above equality is satisfied on the entire
interval J: consequently, ηi is a Nash function by Proposition 8.1.8 of [3] and η : J →Rn is an
injective Nash arc. The rest of the proof now runs as in the proof of lemma 9.6.8: by hypothesis
on S, we have η(J) ⊂S and γ(I) ⊂S, and therefore S is arc-symmetric in Rn.
Before restricting our attention to semialgebraic arc-symmetric sets, let us state the follow-
ing property that make the closed (for Euclidean topology) arc-symmetric subsets of Rn into
the closed sets of a topology on Rn:
Proposition 9.6.10. Any intersection or finite union of closed arc-symmetric subsets of Rn
is a closed arc-symmetric subset of Rn.
Proof. Let S, T ⊂Rn be closed (with respect to Euclidean topology) arc-symmetric in Rn and
let γ :] −1; 1[→Rn be an analytic arc such that γ(] −1; 0[) is contained in the closed set S ∪T.
If the interior of γ−1(S) in ] −1; 1[ is non-empty, then γ(] −1; 1[) ⊂S ⊂S ∪T since S is arc-
symmetric. Suppose that the interior of γ−1(S) is empty, then the interior of γ−1(T) in ]−1; 1[
is necessarily non-empty. Indeed, if the interior of γ−1(T) was also empty, then the interior
of γ−1(S ∪T) = γ−1(S) ∪γ−1(T) would be empty as well because S and T (and then γ−1(S)
and γ−1(T)) are closed, but ] −1; 0[⊂γ−1(S ∪T). As a consequence, since T is arc-symmetric,
γ(] −1; 1[) ⊂T ⊂S ∪T.
Now, let (Si)i∈I be a family of closed arc-symmetric subsets of Rn and let γ :] −1; 1[→Rn
be an analytic arc such that γ(] −1; 0[) is contained in the closed set T
i∈I Si i.e. for all i ∈I,

i ∈I i.e. γ(] −1; 1[) ⊂T

Remark 9.6.11. If S is an arc-symmetric set of Rn and T is an arc-symmetric set of an affine
space Rm, then the Cartesian product S × T is an arc-symmetric set of Rn+m.
Indeed, if
γ :] −1; 1[→Rn+m is an analytic arc such that γ(] −1; 0[) ⊂S × T and if we write γ = (γ1, γ2)
with γ1 :] −1; 1[→Rn and γ2 :] −1; 1[→Rm, we have γ1(] −1; 0[) ⊂S and γ2(] −1; 0[) ⊂T
and then, since S and T are both arc-symmetric, γ1(] −1; 1[) ⊂S and γ2(] −1; 1[) ⊂T i.e.
γ(] −1; 1[) ⊂S × T.

9.6.2
Semialgebraic arc-symmetric sets of real affine spaces and the AR topology
From now on, we will focus on semialgebraic arc-symmetric sets of Rn. First, remark that all
semialgebraic arc-symmetric sets are closed (this is Remarque 1.3 of [15] and Proposition 2.8
of [16]):
Proposition 9.6.12. Let S be a semialgebraic arc-symmetric subset of Rn. Then S is closed
with respect to the Euclidean topology of Rn.
Proof. Let x be a point of the Euclidean closure S of S. Since S is semialgebraic, by Nash
curve selection lemma (Proposition 8.1.13 of [3]), there exists a Nash arc γ :] −1; 1[→Rn such
that γ(0) = x and γ(]0; 1[) ⊂S. Because S is arc-symmetric in Rn, we have γ(] −1; 1[) ⊂S, in
particular x = γ(0) ∈S.
One part of this section will be dedicated to the proof of the following result: the semi-
algebraic arc-symmetric subsets of Rn are the closed sets of a Noetherian topology on Rn
(Th´eor`eme 1.4 of [15] and Theorem 2.12 of [16]).
Theorem 9.6.13. Any intersection or finite union of semialgebraic arc-symmetric subsets
of Rn is a semialgebraic arc-symmetric subset of Rn. Furthermore, the topology on Rn whose
closed sets are exactly the semialgebraic arc-symmetric subsets of Rn is a Noetherian topology.
Since any semialgebraic arc-symmetric subset of Rn is closed by previous proposition 9.6.12,
any intersection or finite union of semialgebraic arc-symmetric subsets of Rn is arc-symmetric
in Rn by proposition 9.6.10. Because any finite union of semialgebraic sets is (by definition) a
semialgebraic set, the proof of the first statement of theorem 9.6.13 amounts to show that any
intersection of semialgebraic arc-symmetric subsets of Rn is a semialgebraic subset of Rn. We
are actually going to see that an intersection of semialgebraic arc-symmetric subsets of Rn is
necessarily a finite one:
Lemma 9.6.14. Let M be a connected Nash submanifold of Rn.
1. If S is a semialgebraic arc-symmetric subset of Rn, then M ⊂S or dim(M ∩S) < dim M.
2. If (Si)i∈I is a family of semialgebraic arc-symmetric subsets of Rn, then there exist
i1, . . . , ik ∈I such that

Proof.
1. We follow the proofs of Lemme 1.5 of [15] and Lemma 2.14 of [16]. Denote by d the
dimension of M (as a semialgebraic set and a submanifold of Rn: see Proposition 2.8.14
of [3]) and suppose that d = dim(M ∩S). Then, by remark 9.6.15 below, there exists a
non-empty open subset of M which is contained in M ∩S i.e. the interior U of M ∩S
in M is non-empty. Let us show that U is furthermore closed in M: let x be an element
of the closure of U in M (i.e. an element of M and the Euclidean closure of U in Rn).
Since M is a Nash submanifold of dimension d of Rn, there exists a Nash diffeomorphism ϕ

and x ∈U. As a conclusion, U ⊂M ∩S is an open and closed subset of M, which is
connected, and therefore U = M ∩S = M.

Definition 9.6.16. The topology on Rn whose closed sets are exactly the semialgebraic arc-
symmetric subsets of Rn is called the AR topology on Rn. If S is a subset of Rn, the closure
of S with respect to AR topology is denoted by S

Since the AR topology of Rn is Noetherian, any non-empty AR-closed subset of Rn (by
definition, a subset of Rn is AR-closed if and only if it is a semialgebraic arc-symmetric subset
of Rn) has a unique finite decomposition into irreducible AR-closed components that we will
call its AR-irreducible components. Furthermore:
Proposition 9.6.18. Let S be an irreducible AR-closed set of Rn. Then S is connected with
respect to Euclidean topology.
Furthermore, the Zariski closure S

Proof. Let S1, . . . , Sk be the connected components of the AR-irreducible semialgebraic arc-
symmetric set S. The sets S1, . . . , Sk are semialgebraic (see Theorem 2.4.5 of [3]) and arc-
symmetric (by remark 9.6.7). Since S writes as the disjoint union Fk

and is AR-irreducible, we have k = 1 and S = S1 is connected.

9.6.3
AR-irreducible components of real algebraic sets
Let us dedicate one part of this section to the description of the AR-irreducible components
of maximal dimension of the real algebraic subsets of Rn. First, consider the nonsingular case:
Lemma 9.6.19. Let X ⊂Rn be a nonsingular real algebraic set. Any connected component
of X is an irreducible AR-closed subset of Rn. As a consequence, the decomposition of X into
connected components is the decomposition of X into its AR-irreducible components.
Proof. Let C be a connected component of X (C is then an AR-closed set of Rn by exam-
ple 9.6.4 2) and suppose that C is a union S1 ∪S2 of semialgebraic arc-symmetric subsets S1
and S2 of Rn such that C ̸= S2. Since X is nonsingular, C is a Nash submanifold of dimension
d := dim X of Rn (see Propositions 3.3.11 and 2.8.14 of [3]) and then, because C is further-
more connected, we have dim S2 < d by lemma 9.6.14 1. The dimension of S1 is therefore
necessarily equal to d and, by the same statement of lemma 9.6.14, C = S1. As a result, C
is AR-irreducible.
Remark 9.6.20. The statement of lemma 9.6.19 is not true for singular real algebraic sets:
if X is for instance the Cartan umbrella of example 9.6.6, X is connected and writes as the
union of the canopy S and the handle T of the umbrella (cf. example 9.6.6), which are both
semialgebraic arc-symmetric in R3 (recall also that X is Zariski-irreducible).
The general case is a consequence of the following result. For its proof, we refer to the proof
of Theorem 2.21 of [16].

9.6.4
Arc-symmetric sets and arc-analytic maps
In this part, we study the links between arc-symmetric sets and arc-analytic maps: let m ∈

Definition 9.6.26. Let S be a subset of Rn, T be a subset of Rm and f : S →T be a map. We
say that f is arc-analytic if for any analytic arc γ :]−1; 1[→S, the composition f◦γ :]−1; 1[→T
is analytic.

Remark 9.6.28. By arguments similar to the ones we used in the proof of lemma 9.6.3, a map f
from a subset S of Rn to a subset T of Rm is arc-analytic if and only if for any analytic arc
γ :]ϵ; ϵ′[→S, the composition f ◦γ :]ϵ; ϵ′[→T is analytic.
Since analytic arcs come up in the definition of both notions, we can expect that arc-
symmetric sets and arc-analytic maps are somehow “compatible”. The proposition below is a
first result in this direction (see also Proposition 5.1 of [15] and Proposition 2.31 of [16]):
Proposition 9.6.29. Let S be a subset of Rn, T be a subset of Rm and f : S →T be an
arc-analytic map.
1. If S is an arc-symmetric set of Rn and T is an arc-symmetric set of Rm, the graph
Γf := {(x, y) ∈S × T | y = f(x)} of f is an arc-symmetric set of Rn+m.
2. If S is an arc-symmetric set of Rn and A is any arc-symmetric set of Rm included in T,
then f−1(A) is an arc-symmetric set of Rn.
Proof.
1. Suppose that S is an arc-symmetric set of Rn and T is an arc-symmetric set of Rm.
Let γ = (γ1, γ2) :]−1; 1[→Rn+m = Rn ×Rm be an analytic arc such that γ(]−1; 0[) ⊂Γf
i.e.
for all t ∈] −1; 0[, γ1(t) ∈S, γ2(t) ∈T and γ2(t) = f ◦γ1(t).
First, we have
γ1(] −1; 1[) ⊂S, resp. γ2(] −1; 1[) ⊂T, since S, resp. T, is arc-symmetric. Moreover,
since f ◦γ1 :] −1; 1[→Rm is an analytic function (because f is arc-analytic), we have
γ2 = f ◦γ1 on the entire interval ] −1; 1[ (by the identify theorem for analytic functions)
and then γ(] −1; 1[) ⊂Γf.
2. Suppose that S is an arc-symmetric set of Rn, let A be an arc-symmetric set of Rm
included in T and let γ :] −1; 1[→Rn be an analytic arc such that γ(] −1; 0[) ⊂f−1(A)
i.e. γ(] −1; 0[) ⊂S and f ◦γ(] −1; 0[) ⊂A. Since S is arc-symmetric, γ(] −1; 1[) ⊂S
and, since f ◦γ :] −1; 1[→Rm is an analytic arc (because f is arc-analytic) and A is
arc-symmetric, we have f ◦γ(] −1; 1[) ⊂A i.e. γ(] −1; 1[) ⊂f−1(A).
As in [15] and [16], let us now focus on semialgebraic arc-symmetric sets and semialgebraic
arc-analytic maps:

Proposition 9.6.30. Let S be an AR-closed set of Rn, T be a semialgebraic subset of Rm and
f : S →T be a semialgebraic arc-analytic map.
1. If T is furthermore arc-symmetric in Rm, then the graph Γf := {(x, y) ∈S × T | y = f(x)}
of f is AR-closed in Rn+m and f is furthermore continuous with respect to (the topologies
induced on S and T by) Euclidean topology.

is continuous with respect to (the topologies induced on S and T by) AR topology.

come from the latter together with the very definition of a semialgebraic map and the fact
that the inverse image of a semialgebraic set by a semialgebraic map is a semialgebraic (see for
instance Proposition 2.2.7 of [3]). Assuming that T is arc-symmetric in Rn, it then remains to
show that f is continuous with respect to Euclidean topology.
Since the graph Γf of f is AR-closed in Rn+m, it is in particular closed with respect to

Remark 9.6.31.
If S is any subset of Rn and f : S →Rm is a map such that Γf is closed
and f is locally bounded, then f is continuous (with respect to Euclidean topology). Indeed,

let x ∈S and let (xk)k∈N be a sequence of elements of S converging to x. Let also δ and K be

by the projection map X × Y →X which is closed because Y is compact): since the graph

9.6.5
Arc-symmetric sets of real analytic manifolds
The notion of arc-symmetric set can be generalized to any real analytic manifold: let M be a
real analytic manifold of dimension n ∈N and recall that a continuous arc f : I →M, where I
is an open interval of R, is analytic if and only if for any t ∈I, there exists an analytic chart
ϕ : U ⊂M →Rn of M around f(t) such that the arc ϕ ◦f : f−1(U) →Rn is analytic.
Definition 9.6.35. Let S be a subset of M. We say that S is an arc-symmetric set of M if
for any analytic arc γ :] −1; 1[→M, if γ(] −1; 0[) ⊂S, then γ(] −1; 1[) ⊂S.
Remark that the characterizations of lemma 9.6.3 remain valid if we replace Rn by M
(the proof of lemma 9.6.3 amounts to deal with analytic diffeomorphisms on open intervals).
Let us also highlight a first class of examples of arc-symmetric sets of M, which generalizes
example 9.6.4 1:
Lemma 9.6.36.
Let N be an analytic subset of M i.e. for any x ∈M, there exists an open
neighborhood U of x in M and an analytic function f : U →R such that N ∩U = f−1(0). If N
is closed in M, then N is arc-symmetric in M. In particular, any closed analytic submanifold
of M is arc-symmetric in M.
Proof. Suppose that N is closed in M and let γ :] −1; 1[→M be an analytic arc such that
γ(]−1; 0[) ⊂N: since N is closed and γ is continuous, we have γ(]−1; 0]) ⊂N. Considering an
open neighborhood U of x in M and an analytic function f : U →R such that N ∩U = f−1(0),
we can then follow the arguments of example 9.6.4 1 to show that N is arc-symmetric in M.
In order to prove the second statement of the lemma, let us show that any analytic subman-
ifold of M is an analytic subset of M. Let S be an analytic submanifold of dimension d ∈N
of M: for any point x ∈S, there exists an analytic diffeomorphism ϕ from an open neigh-
borhood U of x in M onto an open neighborhood V of the origin in Rn such that ϕ(x) = 0
and
ϕ(S ∩U) = {(x1, . . . , xn) ∈V | xd+1 = . . . = xn = 0}.
If we denote by πi : V →R the projection on the ith coordinate, i ∈{1, . . . , n}, we have
S ∩U = f−1(0) where f is the analytic function U →R ; y 7→
 π2

argument is identical to example 9.6.4 3).

Remark 9.6.37. The topological arguments of remark 9.6.7 are also valid in this context: the
connected components of an arc-symmetric set of M are arc-symmetric in M.
Considering arc-symmetric sets of real analytic manifolds, it is natural to study the com-
patibility of the notion with analytic diffeomorphisms. But actually, we can already consider
the following generalizations of definition 9.6.26 and proposition 9.6.29:
Definition 9.6.38. Let S be a subset of M, T be a subset of a real analytic manifold N and
f : S →T be a map. We say that f is arc-analytic if for any analytic arc γ :] −1; 1[→S, the
composition f ◦γ :] −1; 1[→T is analytic.
Proposition 9.6.39.
Let S be a subset of M, T be a subset of a real analytic manifold N of
dimension m and f : S →T be an arc-analytic map.
1. If S is an arc-symmetric set of M and T is an arc-symmetric set of N, then the graph
Γf := {(x, y) ∈S × T | y = f(x)} of f is an arc-symmetric set of the product analytic
manifold M × N.
2. If S is an arc-symmetric set of M and A is any arc-symmetric set of N included in T,
then f−1(A) is an arc-symmetric set of M.
In particular, if Φ : N →M is an analytic diffeomorphism, S ⊂M is arc-symmetric in M if
and only if Φ−1(S) is arc-symmetric in N (an analytic map between real analytic manifolds is
arc-analytic).
Proof.
1. Suppose that S is an arc-symmetric set of M. Let γ = (γ1, γ2) :] −1; 1[→M × N
be an analytic arc such that γ(] −1; 0[) ⊂Γf i.e. for all t ∈] −1; 0[, γ1(t) ∈S, γ2(t) ∈T
and γ2(t) = f ◦γ1(t). First, we have γ1(] −1; 1[) ⊂S, resp. γ2(] −1; 1[) ⊂T, since
S is arc-symmetric in M, resp. T is arc-symmetric in N. Furthermore, since the maps
γ2 :] −1; 1[→N and f ◦γ1 :] −1; 1[→N are both analytic maps (f is arc-analytic), they
are in particular continuous so that γ2(0) = f ◦γ1(0). Moreover, if ψ : W →Rm is an
analytic chart on N such that γ2(0) = f ◦γ1(0) ∈W and if ϵ is a positive real number such
that ]−ϵ; ϵ[ is included is the non-empty open subset γ−1
2 (W)∩(f ◦γ1)−1 (W) of ]−1; 1[,
we have ψ ◦γ2 = ψ ◦f ◦γ1 on ]−ϵ; 0], hence on the entire interval ]−ϵ; ϵ[ (by the identify
theorem for analytic functions) and then γ(] −1; ϵ[) ⊂Γf.
2. The proof is identical to the proof of proposition 9.6.29 2.
As a particular case of the last statement of proposition 9.6.39, consider the inclusion map
i : N ,→M of an analytic submanifold N of M: if a subset T of N is arc-symmetric in M, then
T = i−1(T) is arc-symmetric in N. The converse is true if N is a closed analytic submanifold
of M:
Lemma 9.6.40. Let N be a closed analytic submanifold of M and T be a subset of N. Then T
is arc-symmetric in N if and only if T is arc-symmetric in M.

Proof. Suppose that T is arc-symmetric in N and let γ :] −1; 1[→M be an analytic arc

γ(]−1; 1[) ⊂N and we can restrict γ into an arc eγ :]−1; 1[→N, which is analytic with respect
to the real analytic manifold N since N is an analytic submanifold of M. As a consequence,
because T is arc-symmetric in N and eγ(] −1; 0[) = γ(] −1; 0[) ⊂T, we have eγ(] −1; 1[) ⊂T
i.e. γ(] −1; 1[) ⊂T.

9.6.6
Semialgebraic sets of Nash manifolds
As we did in real affine spaces, we would like to consider semialgebraic arc-symmetric sets
in this more general context of real analytic manifolds and we are then led to consider Nash
manifolds. We therefore suppose from now that M is a Nash manifold of dimension n i.e. a
real analytic manifold with respect to a finite atlas of charts ϕ : U →Rn with semialgebraic
open image ϕ(U) in Rn such that the analytic transition maps are Nash diffeomorphisms, i.e.
semialgebraic diffeomorphisms, between semialgebraic open subsets of Rn (such an atlas will be
called a Nash atlas). For instance, the real projective space Pn(R), with its usual analytic atlas,
is a Nash manifold of dimension n. We refer to [25] for a thorough study of Nash manifolds
and we begin this part by recalling below the definition and some properties of semialgebraic
sets of Nash manifold. A Nash chart on M is a continuous map φ : V →Rn from an open
subset V of M which restricts to an homeomorphism φ : V →φ(V ) onto a semialgebraic
open subset of Rn such that for any chart ϕ : U →Rn of the Nash atlas of M, the map
φ ◦ϕ−1 : ϕ(U ∩V ) →φ(U ∩V ) is a Nash diffeomorphism between semialgebraic open subsets
of Rn.
105


## Page 106


Definition 9.6.43. Let S be a subset of M. We say that S is semialgebraic if for any chart
ϕ : U →Rn of the Nash atlas of M, the set ϕ(S ∩U) is a semialgebraic subset of Rn.
Notice that, in particular, M is a semialgebraic subset of M and that, if φ : V →Rn is a
Nash chart on M, V is a semialgebraic subset of M. Remark also that S ⊂M is semialgebraic
in M if and only if for any Nash chart φ : V →Rn on M, φ(S ∩V ) is a semialgebraic subset
of Rn.
Let us now state and show the following stability properties that extend the usual stability
properties of semialgebraic sets of real affine spaces:
Lemma 9.6.44. Any finite Boolean combination of semialgebraic subsets of M is semialgebraic
in M. Moreover, if S is a semialgebraic set of M, the closure and interior of S in M are
semialgebraic in M and, if T is a semialgebraic subset of a Nash manifold N, the Cartesian
product S × T is a semialgebraic subset of the Nash manifold M × N.
Proof. Let A and B be semialgebraic subsets of M and let φ : V →Rn be a Nash chart on M.
The subsets φ((A ∩B) ∩V ) = φ(A ∩V ) ∩φ(B ∩V ), φ((A ∪B) ∩V ) = φ(A ∩V ) ∪φ(B ∩V )
and φ((M \ A) ∩V ) = φ(V ) \ φ(A ∩V ) of Rn are all semialgebraic, so that the subsets A ∩B,
A ∪B and M ⊂A are all semialgebraic in M.
As for the second part of the statement, keep the Nash chart φ : V →Rn on M, denote by S
the closure of S in M and remark that S∩V is the closure of S∩V in V (S∩V is a closed subset
of V containing S ∩V and if x ∈S ∩V and W is any open subset of V containing x, we have
W ∩S ∩V = W ∩S ̸= ∅, since W is also an open neighborhood of x in M). As a consequence,
the set φ
 S ∩V

is the closure of the semialgebraic set φ(S ∩V ) in the semialgebraic open
subset φ(V ) of Rn, which is the intersection of φ(V ) with the closure of φ(S ∩V ) in Rn and
is therefore semialgebraic (see Proposition 2.2.2 of [3]): S is then a semialgebraic subset of M.
The interior of S in M, being the set M \

, is consequently also semialgebraic in M.
In order to show the final statement, let (ϕi : Ui →Rn)i∈{1,...,r} be the Nash atlas of M,
(ψj : Wj →Rm)j∈{1,...,s} be the Nash atlas of N and consider the product Nash manifold M ×N

Some classes of maps are natural to be considered in this context: if N is a Nash manifold
of dimension m and Φ : M →N is a map, we say that Φ is a semialgebraic map, resp. a
Nash map, if for any Nash chart ϕ : U →Rn on M and any Nash chart ψ : W →Rm
on N, the set Φ−1(W) is semialgebraic in M, resp. open semialgebraic in M, and the map

Definition 9.6.46. Let S be a semialgebraic subset of M, T be a semialgebraic subset of a
Nash manifold N of dimension m and f : S →T be a map. We say that f is a semialgebraic
map if for any Nash chart ϕ : U →Rn on M and any Nash chart ψ : W →Rm on N, the set
f−1(T ∩W) is semialgebraic in M and the map ψ ◦f ◦ϕ−1 : ϕ

→Rm is a
semialgebraic map.
Lemma 9.6.47. Let S be a semialgebraic subset of M, T be a semialgebraic subset of a Nash
manifold N of dimension m and f : S →T be a map.
1. The map f is semialgebraic if and only if the graph Γf of f is semialgebraic in M × N.
2. If B is a semialgebraic subset of M included in S, A is a semialgebraic subset of N
included in T and f is a semialgebraic map, then f−1(A) is semialgebraic in M and f(B)
is semialgebraic in N.
3. If f is a bijective semialgebraic map, then its inverse f−1 is a semialgebraic map as well.
Proof. The same arguments as in the proof of lemma 9.6.45 show statements 1 and 2. As for
the third statement, remark that, if f is bijective, the graph Γf−1 of the inverse of f is the
image of Γf by the Nash diffeomorphism M × N →N × M ; (x, y) 7→(y, x).
Remarks 9.6.48. Keep the notations of previous lemma 9.6.47.
1. If B is a semialgebraic subset of M included in S, A is a semialgebraic subset of N
included in T such that f(B) ⊂A and f is a semialgebraic map, then the map ef : B →
A ; x 7→f(x) induced by f is a semialgebraic map as well, since Γ ef = Γf ∩(B × A) is a
semialgebraic set of M × N by lemma 9.6.47 1 and lemma 9.6.44.
2. If X is a semialgebraic subset of a Nash manifold L and h : T →X is a semialgebraic
map, the composition h ◦f : S →X is a semialgebraic map as well, since the graph Γh◦f
of h◦f is the image of the semialgebraic intersection (Γf ×Z)∩(X ×Γh) (lemma 9.6.47 1
and lemma 9.6.44) by the Nash (and then semialgebraic: cf. lemma 9.6.45 2) projection
map M × N × L →M × L ; (x, y, z) 7→(x, z) (this argument is identical to the proof of
Proposition 2.2.6 (i) of [3]).
Let us also mention the following generalization of Proposition 2.9.10 of [3]. A semialgebraic
subset N of M is a Nash submanifold of dimension d of M if for any x ∈N, there exists a
Nash chart ϕ : U →Rn on M such that x ∈U and
ϕ(N ∩U) = {(x1, . . . , xn) ∈ϕ(U) | xd+1 = . . . = xn = 0}
(this is equivalent to say that for any x ∈N, there exists a Nash diffeomorphism ϕ from a
semialgebraic open neighborhood U of x in M onto a semialgebraic open neighborhood V of

on M is a Nash map). Remark that the inverse image of a Nash submanifold of dimension d
by a Nash diffeomorphism is a Nash submanifold of dimension d.

Corollary 9.6.50. Let S be a semialgebraic subset of M. All the connected components of S
are also semialgebraic in M.
Proof. Write S as a disjoint union of connected Nash submanifolds T1, . . . , Ts of M and let
S1, . . . , Sr be the connected components of S. Then, for i ∈{1, . . . , r}, Si is the union of
some Tj’s and is therefore semialgebraic in M.
Remark 9.6.51. Let us mention that a Nash submanifold N of M is not necessarily a Nash
manifold itself: the point is that, even if N can be equipped with an atlas of Nash charts
induced from Nash charts on M making N into a Nash submanifold (N is therefore a real
analytic manifold), this atlas is not necessarily finite, hence is not a Nash atlas. On the other
hand, if N is furthermore compact, a finite atlas can be extracted from the previous one and N
is therefore a Nash manifold in this case.
We can also consider the following natural notion of dimension for a semialgebraic subset
of M: let S be such a set.
Definition 9.6.52. The dimension of S, denoted by dim S, is the maximum of the dimensions
of the semialgebraic sets ϕ(S ∩U) over the Nash charts ϕ : U →Rn of the Nash atlas of M.
Notice that the dimension of S is equal to the maximum of the dimensions of the semialge-
braic sets φ(S ∩V ) over all the Nash charts φ : V →Rn on M. Remark also that the dimension
of a finite union of semialgebraic subsets of M is the maximum of their dimensions, using and
extending Proposition 2.8.5 i) of [3]. In particular, if T is a semialgebraic subset of M included
in S, since S = T ∪(S \ T), we have dim T ≤dim S.
To end this paragraph, let us show three further properties involving dimension:

Lemma 9.6.54. If S is a Nash submanifold of dimension d of M, we have d = dim S.
Proof. Let φ : V →Rn be a Nash chart on M. The set S ∩V is a then a Nash submanifold
of dimension d of M and φ(S ∩V ) is a Nash submanifold of dimension d of Rn (V is open
semialgebraic in M and φ is a Nash diffeomorphism onto its image which is open semialgebraic
in Rn). Consequently, by Proposition 2.8.14 of [3], dim φ(S ∩V ) = d, so that dim S = d.

9.6.7
The AS topology
We are now ready to consider semialgebraic arc-symmetric subsets of M and generalize theo-
rem 9.6.13 to Nash manifolds. First:
Proposition 9.6.56. Let S be a semialgebraic arc-symmetric subset of M. Then S is closed
in M.
Proof. Let x ∈S and let ϕ : U →Rn be a Nash chart on M such that x ∈U: since x ∈S and U
is an open neighborhood of x in M, S∩U ̸= ∅. Now, consider the semialgebraic subset ϕ(S∩U)
of Rn (S is semialgebraic in M). The point ϕ(x) is in the closure of ϕ(S ∩U) in Rn (see the
proof of lemma 9.6.44) and we can then apply Nash curve selection lemma (Proposition 8.1.13
of [3]) to ϕ(S ∩U) and x: there exists a Nash arc γ :] −1; 1[→Rn such that γ(0) = ϕ(x) and
γ(]0; 1[) ⊂ϕ(S ∩U). In particular, 0 belongs to the open subset γ−1(ϕ(U)) of ] −1; 1[ and
there exists ϵ > 0 such that ] −ϵ, ϵ[⊂γ−1(ϕ(U)), so that γ(] −ϵ; 1[) ⊂ϕ(U). We can therefore
110


## Page 111


consider the analytic arc eγ := ϕ−1 ◦γ :] −ϵ; 1[→M on M. Since γ(]0; 1[) ⊂ϕ(S ∩U), we
have eγ(]0; 1[) ⊂S ∩U ⊂S and then, because S is arc-symmetric in M, eγ(] −ϵ; 1[) ⊂S. In
particular, x = eγ(0) ∈S.
As in the case of Rn, the semialgebraic arc-symmetric subsets of the Nash manifold M are
the closed sets of a Noetherian topology on M:
Theorem 9.6.57. Any intersection or finite union of semialgebraic arc-symmetric subsets
of M is a semialgebraic arc-symmetric subset of M. Furthermore, the topology on M whose
closed sets are exactly the semialgebraic arc-symmetric subsets of M is a Noetherian topology.
Analogously to theorem 9.6.13, because of proposition 9.6.56, proposition 9.6.42 and lemma
9.6.44, the following generalization of lemma 9.6.14 will prove theorem 9.6.57:
Lemma 9.6.58. Let N be a connected Nash submanifold of M.
1. If S is a semialgebraic arc-symmetric subset of M, then N ⊂S or dim(N ∩S) < dim N.
2. If (Si)i∈I is a family of semialgebraic arc-symmetric subsets of M, then there exist
